pending UCs. 
XVII. NATIONAL TOBACCO CONTROL PROGRAMME (NTCP) 
Recommendations/Observations  
17.3  The Committee observes that launched as a pil ot project in 12 districts of 6 States in 2007-08, by the end of 2009-10, 
Programme has been extended to only 42 districts of  21 States. It would not be wrong to conclude that the Programme 
continues to be run on a pilot basis even after thr ee years of its launch. This is the position when t he target was to extend the 
coverage to 100 districts by the end of 2008-09. 
Action Taken  

  Pilot phase of NTCP was launched in 2007-08 in 18  Districts covering 9 States. In 2008-09, it has be en upscaled to 24 
Districts covering 12 States. The total financial o utlay for the programme approved in the Eleventh pl an is Rs.182 crore, to be 
implemented in the remaining years of the Eleventh plan in 42 Districts of 21 States within the NRHM f ramework from 2010-
11. The EFC did not agree for expansion of NTCP to more districts during Eleventh Five Year Plan period. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE 
  Perturbed by the snail’s pace of implementation o f the Programme, the Committee had commented that t he 
Department has not moved beyond running the ambitio us Programme as pilot project. Department’s candid admission 
that the EFC did not agree for expansion of NTCP to  more districts during Eleventh Plan period is perhaps not a result 
of financial constraints but appears to be Departme nt’s failure in expanding this Programme in the des ired manner. 
With the recent track record, the Department does not seem to achieve the target of implementing the programme in the 
remaining years of the Eleventh plan period in 42 Districts of 21 States within the NRHM framework from 2010-11. The 
Committee hopes that the Department gears up to the demanding task of expansion of the Programme in th e remaining 
States. For this to happen, it has to achieve the t argets beforehand first and then approach the EFC f or necessary 
clearance for expansion. 
Recommendations/Observations  
17.5   The Committee would like to remind the Depar tment that subsequent to its examination of Demands  for Grants of 
the Ministry for 2008-09, in its 27th Report, it ha d taken a strong view of the fact that despite the ‘in principle’ approval of the 
Planning Commission for setting up of the National Regulatory Authority and Referral Labs for testing Tar and Nicotine 
content and initiation of the process for seeking approval of the competent authority, no progress had been made for quite some 
time. Accordingly, the Committee had called for con certed efforts of the Department to achieve the app roval of the competent 
authority for setting up of the National Regulatory  Authority. However, as per information available w ith the Committee now, 
there has been ‘no’ improvement on this front even after a period of two years. The Committee is dismayed at the Department’s 
attitude in this regard. National Tobacco Regulator y Authority is supposed to be pivotal to the whole Programme to help in 
effective implementation of Tobacco free initiatives. Even the progress w.r.t. setting up of tobacco testing labs has not seen any 
improvement. To aggravate the matter further, the D epartment has not been able to expand the Programme  in more districts of 
the country as scheduled. Even the first Adult Toba cco Survey has been lingering on for quite some tim e. The Committee is 
upset with the way things are being managed under t he Programme. Fixing realistic targets on paper is only the first step 
towards achieving a goal and the Committee does fin d the targets set to be realistic and achievable. T herefore, it is not able to 
comprehend itself with the reasons for the delay in  achieving the targets. The Committee would, theref ore, recommend some 
urgent and concrete action plan in achieving the targets set under the Programme in a time bound manner.  
Action Taken 
/circle6 The modalities of establishment of Central Tobacco  Regulatory Authority are being worked out. 
/circle6 As regards Referral labs, one Apex/research labora tory and five testing laboratories have been identified and tender 
notice has been issued with regard to procurement of equipments for the identified tobacco laboratories. 
/circle6 As regards to expansion of programme, as per CCEA approval dated 28.01.10, State and district level component of 
NTCP are to be integrated with the NRHM framework w.e.f.  2010. 
/circle6 The fieldwork with regard to Global Adult Tobacco Survey (GATS) has been completed by IIPS, Mumbai, which is 
the implementing agency. The final report is under preparation and is likely to be released in August- September, 
2010.  
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee had been critical of the manner in w hich various components of the Programme are allowe d to 
linger on for several years. The Action Taken Note of the Department does not present a forward moveme nt w.r.t. 
various components. Setting up of the National Toba cco Regulatory Authority is still in the pipeline w ith no progress at 
all. No noticeable improvement w.r.t. Setting up of  Referral Labs for testing Tar and Nicotine content s has been made 
and there is every possibility that this target als o would be carried forward in the next Plan period like setting up of the 

National Tobacco Regulatory Authority. Similarly, f inal report of the Global Adult Tobacco Survey (GAT S) is still 
under preparation. With such dismal progress in res pect of the various components, the Committee can o nly conclude 
that some inherent problems are still persisting an d creating roadblocks in the smooth implementation of the 
Programme. The Committee would reiterate that the Department takes some concrete remedial action for achieving the 
various targets set under the Programme. 
XVIII. MEDICAL EDUCATION  
18.2  The Committee is concerned with the fact that  as many as 10 colleges are being considered for wi thdrawal of recognition 
and 15 more being issued show cause notices. From t he large number of colleges caught compromising the  standards while 
being scrutinized, it is clear that all is not well . This clubbed with the fact that these colleges ar e those which have come under 
scheduled scrutiny and there may be many which keep  on functioning until they come under inspection sc rutiny, makes the 
matter more serious. The Committee would like to ha ve a brief about the status of inspection of all th e medical colleges inter-
alia  containing the selection procedure vide  which the colleges are selected for inspection, th e date of establishment of college, 
date of inspections, shortcomings noticed and actio n taken during the last 5 years. The Committee may also be informed of the 
details w. r. t. the progress made for withdrawal o f recognition of 10 colleges, show cause notice to 15 colleges and the court 
cases in respect of the 4 colleges.  
Action Taken  
  Procedure for conducting the inspection by the Medi cal Council of India  Presently, there are about 300 medical 
colleges in the country out of which 229 medical co lleges are recognized and remaining 71 medical coll eges have been 
approved for running MBBS courses and will be consi dered for recognition as per the provisions of Medi cal Council of India 
(MCI) regulations. 
  MCI carries out periodic inspections of medical c olleges as per the provisions of IMC Act and regula tions made 
thereunder. MCI conducted the inspections of medical colleges under section 10A, 11 (2) and 19 of IMC Act for establishment 
of new medical college, renewal of permission, reco gnition of medical colleges and continuance of reco gnition for award of 
MBBS degree. 
  The medical colleges are inspected by the MCI fro m time to time under the following categories: 
(i) The recognition of a medical college is reviewe d after every five years.  
(ii)  The permission of the approved medical colleg es is renewed every year till such time the medical  college is 
recognized. The permission is renewed if it is found during inspection that all the facilities are as per MCI norms. 
(iii)  Those medical colleges which apply for postg raduate courses for the first time after its recognition, are inspected. 
(iv)  A medical college is inspected when there is a complaint with regard to the malpractice for teaching faculty, clinical 
material and infrastructural facilities. 
(v)  Inspection of a medical college is also conduc ted on the direction of High Courts/Supreme Court. 
  After conducting the inspections under the above said provisions of IMC Act, MCI has so far recommended to the Central 
Government for withdrawal of recognition of 10 medi cal colleges and has issued show cause notice to 15  medical colleges in 
the country. A statement showing the present status of above 25 colleges is at Annexure-XI  
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee had called for a status note in res pect of all the colleges inspected by the MCI durin g the last 5 
years inter-alia  containing - the selection procedure vide  which the colleges were selected for inspection, t he date of 
establishment of college, date of inspection(s), sh ortcomings noticed and action taken after such insp ection. The 
Committee is dismayed at the fact that the required information is conspicuously missing in the Action Taken Note. The 
Committee would accordingly like the Department to furnish the requisite information at the earliest. 

XIX. VACANCIES IN CHS CADRE 
Recommendations/Observations  
19.4  The Committee notes with serious concern that  apart from marginal improvement in GDMO sub-cadre,  that too far too 
below any satisfactory level, there is seemingly no  improvement in the filling up of the vacant positi ons across all the sub-
cadres inspite of the sanctioned strength increasing in the three sub-cadres other than GDMO. The Comm ittee is not convinced 
with the Department’s efforts because had the Depar tment made sincere efforts there would have been so me kind of forward 
movement in filling up the vacancies over the years . The Committee cannot make out the delay in making  recruitments by 
UPSC. Promotion vacancies can be filled up by conve ning DPCs. Further, the Committee is unable to unde rstand the delay in 
notifying the revised CHS rules especially when the re is urgency involved in the matter. Temporary and  contractual measures 
can never be a solution in the long run. 
19.5   The Committee would like to be apprised abou t the details of contractual/temporary appointments made under CHS 
for the past one year. The Committee would also like to be furnished with a detailed note in respect of the posts under each sub-
cadre, inter-alia  covering issues like when it approached the UPSC f or making direct recruitments, holding of DPCs, rea sons 
for delay in notifying CHS rules and the efforts made by the Department for early filling up of the vacancies. 
Action Taken  
  Central Health Service is comprised of 4 sub-cadr es namely (i) Teaching; (ii) Non- Teaching; (iii) Public Health; and (iv) 
General Duty Medical Officers. First 3 sub-cadres a re specialist sub-cadres and recruitment in these s ub-cadres are made 
through UPSC by holding Interviews. On the other ha nd, recruitment in GDMO sub-cadre is made through C ombined Medical 
Service Examination conducted every year by the UPSC. The UPSC being a Constitutional Authority follow its own procedure 
and the Ministry has no role in recruitment process  except to make a request to Commission for expedit ing the process of 
recruitment that too in case of recruitment of spec ialists only. UPSC has a fixed schedule for conduct ing examination followed 
by interviews in case of GDMOs. It takes about one and quarter year for completing the recruitment process commencing from 
conducting of written examination to issue of offer s of appointment to the successful candidates. Ther e is no short cut to this 
method of recruitment of GDMOs. Moreover, most of t he selected candidates have been found to be pursui ng Post Graduate 
Courses and they do not join the posts till their PG course is completed. As such joining rate in GDMO sub-cadre is about 25-30 
% thereby causing the continuance of vacancies. 
  Further, since the appointment process takes long  time, the appointments are made on contractual bas is to ensure that the 
patient care does not suffer. The details of contra ctual/temporary appointments made in the year 2009- 10 and the requisitions 
sent to UPSC for direct recruitment in 4 sub-cadres of CHS is given hereunder:- 
  Details of contractual appointment in CHS

  Details of direct recruitment in various sub-cadr e of CHS during 2009-10 are at Annexure-XIII. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  Status of vacancy position in CHS cadre has been e ngaging the attention of the Committee for consider able time. 
The Committee has been informed that the exercise o f completing the recruitment process by UPSC takes about 15 
months. The Committee is constrained to note that e ven this long-drawn exercise has failed to achieve the desired 
results. During 2009-10 against 186 Teaching Posts under CHS cadre 107 Posts were advertised by UPSC a nd only 22 
candidates joined. Similarly, under Public Health, against 28 posts, 15 posts were advertised by UPSC and only 5 
candidates joined. With regard to Non-teaching post s, against the availability of 90 posts, only 24 po sts were advertised 
by UPSC and not even a single candidate joined. Position was no better for GDMO posts. Out of 300 vacancies reported 
to UPSC for CMSE-2008, 241 candidates were recommen ded for posting. Against 142 candidates offered pos ting in 
April-May, 2010, only 3 candidates have joined so f ar. The Committee is extremely unhappy with the pro gress made in 
filling up the vacant posts of CHS cadre. Prolonged  process of recruitment is compounded by another di sturbing trend 
of candidates finally selected not coming forward t o join. This clearly shows that situation is bound to continue to 
remain very discouraging with the result that publi c at large as well as CGHS beneficiaries will conti nue to remain 
deprived of medical facilities. The Committee is of  the view that the nodal Ministry has to play a pro -active role by 
taking up the issue with UPSC for expediting the re cruitment process as well as analyzing the factors responsible for 
such a low level of joining by successful candidates. 
Recommendations/Observations  
19.7   The Committee is happy to note that finally there is some respite in the offing to General Duty  Medical Officers 
with PG qualification. The Committee recommends to the Department to pursue the matter sincerely so that the amendments are 
approved without further delay. The Committee would  like to be apprised about the progress made in thi s regard. The 
Committee would also like to have the details of the DACP scheme. 
Action Taken  
  The reply is at Annexure-XlV.  
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee observes that the revised draft of CH C Rules after concurrence of DoPT was forwarded to UPSC 
on 14th July, 2009 for approval. Since then, a numb er of queries have been raised by UPSC at different  points of time 
which have been duly clarified by the Department. L ast such clarification was sought by UPSC on 15th M arch, 2010. 
Since then, although almost nine months have passed , the matter hangs with the CHC Rules remaining unr evised. As a 
result, the process of creation of required posts i n different categories as well as modifications in eligibility criteria and 
other allied aspects is being unduly delayed. The C ommittee would appreciate if this matter is vigorou sly pursued, if 
need be at the highest level.

exercise for some of which started even before the beginning of the Plan period. The Committee is cons trained to observe that 
against the approved outlay of Rs. 12609.63 crore f or the Eleventh Plan, very meagre allocation has be en made for the first 
three years which has also remained almost unutiliz ed. Status by the end of 2009-10 in respect of thes e initiatives/programmes 
clearly indicates that there is little hope of their taking off in the real sense by the end of Eleventh Plan, i.e.  2011-12. Following 
details are self-revealing:

Action Taken  
(i)  Telemedicine- In order to prepare the Detailed Project Report fo r the purpose of formulating the EFC proposal for 
telemedicine and e-health projects and EFC note bas ed on the Detailed Project Report, several consulta tions were held 
with experts from time to time as the project is unique and an innovative in nature. This included a meeting with National 
Institute of Smart Government, Hyderabad on 4.11.09  which is supporting several Government departments  on e-
Governance initiatives. In the meanwhile, the work allocation of officers were re-allocated and a new officers were given 
this subject. Since the same was new to them, it to ok time to finalize the Detailed Project Report whi ch is under 
preparation. Another meeting was held on 21.12.2009 by inviting other agencies alongwith experts in this regard. A brain 
storming workshop was also held on 14.1.2010 by inviting a number of experts from governmental and non-governmental 
sectors. A meeting was also held under chairmanship of Secretary (H&FW) on 1.2.2010 and again on 3rd March, 2010. It 
was decided to set up a small Committee which will finalize the Concept Note on Telemedicine to be implemented during 
11th Five Year Plan. This Committee held a meeting on 9th March, 2010 and on 16th March, 2010. There will be another 
meeting to give final shape of this project. The report of the Committee will be submitted shortly. 
   Accordingly the Detailed Project Report and EFC note will be finalized very soon and the funds will  be utilized in 
the beginning of the next financial year. 
(ii)   Diabetes ----  The National Programme for Prevention and Control of Diabetes, Cardiovascular Diseases and Stroke 
(NPDCS) is a new initiative in the 11th Five Year P lan. Rs.1660.50 crore had been earmarked for this purpose during the 
11th Plan Period. Though the Pilot Scheme on NPDCS was launched in 10 States with one district each, t he National 
level Programme could not be launched essentially b ecause the process of consultation was more extensi ve than 
envisaged. A very large number of stakeholders, exp erts and others were consulted. Based on this exerc ise, the NPDCS 
document was prepared. 
  Present Status -  The Expenditure Finance Committee considered and a pproved the NPDCS at an estimated cost of 
Rs.499.38 crore on 8th March, 2010 and Ministry of Health and Family Welfare is moving a note for the Cabinet 
Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA) for its final approval. The programme will be taken up during the  remaining 
period of the 11th FYP, once approval of the Cabinet is obtained and other formalities are finalised.  
(iii)   Organ Transplant -  The SFC document for ‘Action Plan’ for 2010-11 and 2011-12 for implementations of Transplant of 
Human Organs Act (THOA) amendments has been sent to  Planning Commission and Finance Ministry. SFC meet ing 
would be held only after their comments are received. 

(iv)   Oral Health -  An amount of Rs.25 crore has been allotted in the 11th Five Year Plan for a Pilot Project. SFC on the same 
is being drafted with the aim to reduce orodental disease burden and integrate oral health with general health. The project 
emphasise the following objectives:- 
/circle6 Generating awareness among community regarding ora l health problems and their prevention by promoting oral 
public health, IEC, research and school oral health care program. 
/circle6 Strengthening Government Health Systems and develo ping and training adequate Manpower for service delivery. 
  During the year 2010-11 an amount or Rs.3.5 crore  has been provided for the above programme. Modalities 
for merging components of the scheme that can be taken up under NRHM are being worked out. 
(iv)   National Programme for Health Care of Elderly (NP HCE) -  National Programme for Health Care of the Elderly 
(NPHCE) is a new initiative in the 11th Five Year P lan to address various health related problems of e lderly people. 
Rs.400 crore has been earmarked for this purpose during the Plan period. 
   After detailed deliberations with subject expert s and the concerned Departments/Ministries, the Min istry of Health 
and Family Welfare has formulated a draft National Programme for Health Care of the Elderly (NPHCE) at  an estimated 
outlay of Rs.288 crore of which the Central share w ill be Rs. 240 crores and that of States Rs. 48 cro res. The programme 
will initially be implemented in 16 States/UTs with 8 medical institutions and 80 districts of the country. 
   The NPHCE envisages dedicated healthcare facilit ies to the elderly at all levels of healthcare deli very system along 
with provision for long term and short term trainin g to the healthcare Professionals to address the pr oblem of health 
manpower shortage in the field of Geriatrics. 
   The proposal has been approved and recommended b y the Expenditure Finance Committee on 8th March, 2010 and 
the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare has moved for final approval of the programme by the Minister of Finance. 
   The programme will be taken up during the remain ing period of the 11th FYP, once approval of the Fi nance 
Minister is obtained and other formalities are finalised. 
(v)   Proposed upgradation of NICD into NCDC -  It is a new initiative under Central Sector. An ou tlay of Rs.450.00 crores 
has been provided in the 11th Five Year Plan (2007-2012) for upgradation of National Institute of Communicable Disease 
(NICD). During the current Annual Plan, 2010-11 a p rovision of Rs.18.69 crores (Revenue: Rs.6.20 crore s and Capital: 
Rs.12.49 crores) has been provided. The facilities that would accrue after the upgradation has been co mpleted would 
enable the Institute to undertake all the diagnosti c support services including development of rapid d iagnostic tools. The 
proposed upgradation envisages creation of newer ce ntres, newer divisions and up-scaling of the existi ng ones to cope 
with the ever increasing horizon and magnitude of emerging, re-emerging and new diseases. 
   The Institute had got its independent appraisal done as advised by the Planning Commission. The Dep artment of 
Management Studies, IIT Delhi carried out evaluatio n during November, 2007 and submitted its report in  May, 2008. In 
addition, MIS HSCC was appointed as Consultant for preparing Detailed Project Report (DPR). They submitted DPR-1 in 
November, 2009. Based on the above inputs and also detailed consultations at different levels, includi ng with technical 
officers, a draft Memo for Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) was prepared. 
   The EFC is under consideration in the Ministry. 
(vi)   Setting up of Paramedical institutions -  Cabinet approved the proposal on 23.06.2010. Furth er modalities for 
implementation of the scheme are being worked out. 
(vii)   Strengthening/upgradation of pharmacy Schools - SFC is under preparation. 
(viii)   Upgradation/strengthening of Nursing Services -  Cabinet approved the scheme. Guidelines of the sch eme have 
been forwarded to the States for approval. The States have been asked to forward proposals accordingly. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  

  Latest feedback on a number of initiatives and new programmes to be launched during the Eleventh Plan clearly 
indicates that all these proposed ventures are at t he initial stage so far as completion of various fo rmalities are 
concerned. Apprehensions made by the Committee last  year about their progress stand confirmed. The Com mittee has 
no other option but to conclude that chances are ve ry dim so far as launching of these programme durin g Twelfth Plan 
Period are concerned. The Committee, therefore, rec ommends that all these new programmes should be mon itored 
vigorously for their initiation at the earliest. 
XXI.  MISCELLANEOUS 
Recommendations/Observations  
21.2  The Committee opines that seven years is a lo ng period and during this period several drugs woul d have become 
necessary for inclusion or deletion as per practica l requirements. It is not happy with the delay in t he revision of the List. It 
recommends an early updation of the List. The Commi ttee may also be informed when did the updation pro cess of NEDL 
started and whether there is a set time gap prescribed for initiating revision of the List. The Committee may also be informed of 
the progress made in this regard.  
 
Action Taken  
  Preparation of National Essential Drug List of Indi a - ‘National List of Essential Medicine’ was first ti me introduced 
in the country in year 1996 by the Ministry of Heal th and Family Welfare consisting of drugs considere d adequate to meet the 
common contemporary health needs of the general pop ulation and general obligation of the health admini strator to ensure 
abundant availability of such drugs in the country. 
  The National Essential Drug List 1996 was revised  in 2003 and 354 drug formulations covered under the list. The list was 
revised by an expert committee constituted by Direc torate General of Health Services in June, 2002 con sisting of expert of 
various medical specialities. The list was approved by the Ministry in July, 2003. 
  For further revision of the list of essential med icine, a proposal to set up an expert committee has  been forwarded by the 
Office of DCG (I) to the Ministry with the approval of the DGHS. The matter is under consideration of the Ministry of Health. 
  It may however be stated that a collaborative wor kshop was conducted by CDSCO in collaboration with AIIMS in 
September, 2009 which discussed the National Essent ial List 2003. The recommendations of the workshop could also be 
considered by the Committee during its deliberations. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The ‘National List of Essential Medicine’ intends to have some noble objective of ensuring abundant availability of 
drugs considered adequate to meet the common contem porary health needs of the general population in th e country. 
The Committee is happy to note that the same is und er consideration of the Department. The Committee r ecommends 
early consideration and clearance of the same by the Department. The Department’s reply is, however, silent on the time 
gap for such exercise. If such a time lag has not b een decided for this exercise of updation of the Li st, the Committee 
would recommend to the Department to work out in-pr inciple time lag for mandatory updation of the List  in future. 
The Committee may be informed about the progress made in this regard.  
Recommendations/Observations  
21.5   The Committee takes note of a recent Delhi H igh Court directive given on 23rd March, 2007 where by private 
hospitals which were allotted cheap or free land by Government agencies are required to provide free treatment to poor patients 
at the rate of 10% in the Indoor Patient Department  (IPD) and 25% in the Outdoor Patient Department (O PD) of their total 
respective treatment capacities. Hospitals to whom land was allotted on institutional/concessional rat es have been directed to 
provide 10% of the total beds free for poor (whose family income is Rs.4, 000/- or less per month) and free treatment to 25% of 
total patients free in the OPD in pursuance of Hon’ ble High Court orders dated 22.3.2007 and 17.7.2007 . Three hospitals 
namely, Rajiv Gandhi Cancer Institute, St. Stephens  Hospital and Mool Chand Khairati Ram Hospital are not providing free 

treatment stating that they are not covered under H igh Court orders. Ten other hospitals having 171 be ds have approached 
Hon’ble Supreme Court against the orders of Hon’ble High Court and have got interim stay with regard to providing free X-ray, 
CT Scan, Diagnostics etc. However, they are providi ng free treatment as in accordance with orders of H on’ble Supreme Court. 
Remaining 25 hospitals are providing free treatment  as per direction of Hon’ble Supreme Court. The Com mittee may be 
informed about the progress w.r.t.  the case filed by ten hospitals in Hon’ble Supreme Court. 
Action Taken  
  The matter is still sub-judice  
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee is aware that the matter is still sub-judice . However, the Committee recommends to the 
Department to pursue the pending matter in the cour t vigorously so that it does not remain entangled i n prolonged 
litigation and the poor patients are deprived of mu ch needed healthcare needs. The Committee would app reciate if the 
present status of the case is submitted to it alongwith steps taken by the Department to expedite its early resolution. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
21.7   The Committee is pained by the fact that the  poor patients have to pay for the branded name of the medicines. This 
state of affairs is leading the poor patients to hu ge financial debts. Besides, procurement of the bra nded/generic branded 
medicines for Government hospitals, dispensaries an d CGHS leads to heavy burden on the Exchequer compr ising of precious 
taxpayer’s money. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Department to make it mandatory for the Gov ernment hospitals 
and CGHS to procure only generic medicines so that their procurement cost is reduced substantially wit hout compromising on 
the quality and efficacy of such medicines. The Com mittee further recommends the Department to initiat e some measures so 
that generic medicines are not marketed and sold as  generic branded medicines leading to exorbitant pr ices by the profiteering 
companies so that such medicines are available to the common man at reasonable prices. The Committee also recommends that 
it should be made mandatory for the Government doctors to prescribe only generic medicines. The Committee may be informed 
of the action taken by the Department in this regard. 
Action Taken 
  Branded Medicine, Generic Medicine, Branded Gener ic Medicines - The Committee has recommended that the use of 
generics in the public as well as private sectors w ill reduce the drug costs and increase drug availab ility. Competitive bulk 
procurement in generic name will help in procuring drugs at competitive prices. 
  It may be stated that earlier the Drugs and Cosme tics Rules, 1945 were amended vide  GSR 27(E) dated 17.01.1981 and 
the following Rule was inserted under rule 96 on labelling of drugs: 
  "The following preparations shall be labelled onl y with proper name of the drug and not with any trade name: 
(1) preparations containing any new drug as the sin gle active ingredient and approved under Rule 30-A,  69-B or 75-B 
by the Licensing Authority subject to the condition that such preparations should be marketed under a generic name 
only. 
(2) Preparations containing any drug specified in S chedule W as the single active ingredient." 
  At that time there were five drugs namely Analgin , Aspirin and its salts, Chlorpromazine and its sal ts, Ferrous Sulphate 
and Pipeprazine and its salts in Schedule W which w ere required to be marketed under generic names onl y. M/s. Heochst 
Pharmaceuticals filed a petition in the High Court challenging the above rule and Schedule W. The prov ision permiting the 
marketing of the five drugs in Schedule W only if l abelled only with the generic name was struck down by the High Court as 
arbitrary. High Court also directed to amend the no tification, with liberty to require the manufacture rs to prominently display, 
apart from the trade name of the drug, its generic name on the labels, containers or bottles (Union of  India v. Hoechst 
Pharmaceuticals Ltd., 1999 SCC (Cri) 138. 

  In compliance to the directions of the Court, the  said rule and Schedule W was omitted by the Minist ry of Health and 
Family Welfare vide  notification GSR 94(E) dated 08.02.2000. 
  At present there is no provision under Drugs and Cosmetics Act and Rules which makes it mandatory th at medicines 
should be marketed under generic name only without any brand name. Manufacturer or importer is free to  market their product 
either under a generic name only or under a brand name. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE 
  The Committee recommends to the Department to ini tiate suitable action to amend the Drugs and Cosmet ics Act 
and Rules so that generic medicines are not allowed  to be sold as branded medicines for the sake of pr ofiteering. The 
Committee would also recommend to the Department to  ensure availability of generic medicines in the op en market. 
The Committee would like to be informed about the action taken by the Department in this regard.  
Recommendations/Observations  
21.8  The Committee’s attention has been drawn to t he Mukhyamantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana being run in the State of 
Rajasthan which gives healthcare coverage to depriv ed sections of the society. The Committee feels tha t such welfare schemes 
should be encouraged in all possible ways including extending central funding for the same. The Committee may be apprised of 
the Department’s view in this regard. 
Action Taken  
  Health being ‘State’ subject, it is the prerogati ve of respective State Government to take initiatives as per the needs of that 
State and availability of resources. In so far as M /O Health and Family Welfare is concerned, no propo sal is presently under 
consideration for providing central funding for State-specific Schemes. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  While appreciating a scheme for the healthcare cove rage for the deprived sections of the society by th e State of 
Rajasthan, the Committee had called for encouraging  such initiatives in different States in all possib le ways including 
extending central funding for the same. The Departm ent’s cold response to this suggestion by terming “ health” as a 
State subject and stating that there was no proposa l for providing central funding for the State-speci fic subjects is 
nothing but outrightly rejecting a good initiative on technical grounds. Had the Department been serio us on this 
suggestion, it would have seized the initiative and started a similar initiative at the Central level whereby all other States 
could be funded from the central pool for undertaking similar initiatives for the poor people.

I.  BUDEGETARY PROVISIONS 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.2   The Committee has been informed that against the projected demand for Rs.19,989.00 crore for NRH M, the Planning 
Commission has approved an outlay of Rs.15440.00 cr ore for 2010-11. The lower approved outlay as compa red to proposed 
outlay is likely to affect implementation of a numb er of ongoing schemes under NRHM like Mission Flexi ble Pool, RCH 
Flexible Pool, Infrastructure Maintenance, National  Vector Borne Disease Control Programme, Revised Na tional TB 
Programme, Management Information System and Routin e Immunization. The Committee would, however, like to point out 
that under all such programmes which are going to b e adversely affected due to non-allocation of requi red funds, funds 
allocated so far have remained underutilized during 2009-10, as indicated below:- Annexure-C of Report.  
Action Taken  
  The proposed and approved outlay for National Rur al Health Mission (NRHM) is Rs.19989 crores and Rs. 15440 crores 
respectively. Keeping in view the inter-sectoral priorities within the NRHM, the outlay has been distributed among the different 
schemes. The lower approved outlay as compared to proposed outlay is likely to affect implementation of a number of ongoing 
schemes in the NRHM like Mission Flexible Pool, RCH  Flexible Pool, Infrastructure Maintenance, Nationa l Vector Borne 
Disease Control Programme, Revised National Tubercu losis Control Programme, Management Information Sys tem and 
Routine Immunization. A mid-year exercise is propos ed to be carried out to assess the requirement of f unds required by the 
schemes/programmes based on the activities planned and progress made therein and supplementary, if req uired, will be sought 
from Ministry of Finance. 
  The allocation and expenditure under the above sc hemes during 2009-10 are given in the Annexure-II.  
  It may be seen from the statement that in most of  the programmes there has been considerable progres s in so far as 
utilization of funds is concerned. This has been possible due to increased absorptive capacity of the States. 
  The efforts will continue to be made by the Minis try to further increase the absorptive capacity of States by removing the 
existing constraints. 

Recommendations/Observations  
2.3   Not only has this, with the exception of Miss ion and RCH Flexible Pool in other programmes, posi tion has been more or 
less the same during 2007-08 and 2008-09 also. Comm ittee’s attention has also been drawn by the fact t hat provisions for all 
these programmes have been made at an enhanced leve l in 2010-11 when compared with 2009-10 allocations . The Committee 
would, therefore, appreciate if concerted efforts a re made by all concerned for optimum and judicious utilization of allocated 
funds. If required, at the time of mid-year assessm ent, the Department can pursue for additional funds  with the Planning 
Commission and Ministry of Finance. 
Action Taken  
  Noted for compliance. 
III.  INFRASTRUCTURE STRENGTHENING  
3.3  On a query about the progress in the construct ion work of sub-centres under the ‘Infrastructure S trengthening Project’ of 
NRHM, the Committee was informed that the progress of Sub-Centre construction is being followed on priority basis to ensure 
functional facilities at Sub-Centre level across th e country at the earliest. As per RHS-2008, out of total 1,46,036 Sub-Centres 
functioning across the country, a total of 78,803 w ere functioning in Government buildings, and 9684 S Cs were being 
constructed across the country. The Committee was given to understand that estimated cost of construction is Rs.968.4 crores at 
the rate of Rs.10 lakhs for 9684 sub-centres, for which funds were released through NRHM Flexible Pool. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.7   Another cause of serious concern for the Comm ittee is the lack of basic infrastructural facilities noticed by the test checks 
carried out by the CAG. Following details are self-revealing:- 
ANNEXURE-E OF REPORT 
  The Committee would like to point out that Perfor mance Audit by CAG covered all the 33 States/UTs wi th 123 districts 
covering 2369 health centres. These 123 districts w ere selected from all the States. In each selected district - 3 CHCs, 6 PHCs 
and 12 Sub-Centres were audited. The Committee, taking note of CAG findings, in these 123 districts, apprehends that position 
would be more or less the same in the remaining districts of the country. 
Action Taken  
  Point noted for action. 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.8  The Committee would like to point out that one  of the key objectives of NRHM is to provide improv ed health care 
services through creation of adequate number of SCs , PHCs and CHCs. However, this basic goal of NRHM h as failed to show 
the desired results. This has been amply proved in the latest Performance Audit of NRHM conducted by t he CAG. The 
Committee has also been expressing grave concern re garding lack of adequate rural health infrastructur e, especially in High 
Focus States from time to time. Even though NRHM ha s completed its 5th year since commencement, much r emains to be 
accomplished so far as infrastructure development i s concerned. With two more years of the Mission per iod remaining, the 
Committee wonders if the targets to overcome the sh ortfall of Health Care Infrastructure would be met in the real sense. The 
Committee, therefore, once again urges the Departme nt to quicken the pace of implementation so that it  does not remain very 
far off from the targeted goals.  
Action Taken  

  The NRHM promotes creation of new health infrastr ucture. It, however, is trying to consolidate exist ing health 
infrastructure before focusing only on creation of new infrastructure as human resource has to be adequately provided wherever 
physical infrastructure is developed. 
IV.   MISSION FLEXIBLE POOL 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.22   Role of ASHA  
  The Committee notes that the major job responsibi lities of ASHA are :- 
– awareness generation on Health, RCH, HIV/AIDS amo ng the people. 
– advising newly married couples for their special needs 
– providing escort services to and guiding pregnant  women for availing of institutional facilities for Anti-Natal, Natal 
and Post-Natal needs 
– counselling on nutrition, pregnancy, breast-feedi ng, immunization and family planning 
– establishing link with PHCs, private providers an d others for service requirement of beneficiaries 
– mobilising parents for child survival interventio ns 
– promoting basic sanitization 
– provision of non-clinical spacing contraceptives 
– surveillance of communicable diseases. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record.  
Recommendations/Observations  
4.23   Apart from all these, ASHA is also the prima ry interface conferred with the duty of detection, follow up and 
counseling of patients for all National Health Prog rammes run under the banner of NRHM. She is the Dep ot Holder for a 
number of health commodities like Vitamin A, Impreg nated bed nets, ORS etc. She is also supposed to ha ndle treatment of 
minor ailments and use the Drug Kit assigned to her  effectively for preliminary investigations. Anothe r major task assigned to 
ASHA is to provide escort services for women seekin g delivery services at institutions, requiring emer gency care and MTP 
services, women and men for clinical contraception services and VCTC. The Committee would like to note  that it had raised 
serious apprehensions regarding the effectivity of ASHA as a multipurpose social health activist as envisaged under the NRHM, 
given the increasing number of job responsibilities  that she was being burdended with, in its 27th Rep ort on Demands for 
Grants 2008-09. The Committee had also voiced its c oncerns on the remuneration package of the ASHA vis-a-vis  the various 
activities she was to be associated with, and had a ccordingly recommended that the role of ASHA needs to be reassessed and 
reformulated. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
VIII.  NATIONAL LEPROSY ERADICATION PROGRAMME 
Recommendations/Observations  
8.1  The goal of leprosy elimination at the Nationa l level is less than 1 case per 10000 population. N ational level elimination 
of Leprosy has been achieved since 31st December, 2 005 when the prevalence rate came down to 0.95 or l ess than 1/10000 

population. 32 States/ UTs have achieved the status of leprosy elimination. Only 3 States/ UT i.e.  Bihar, Chhattisgarh and Dadra 
and Nagar Haveli are yet to achieve elimination level, whith have the prevalence rate of 1.07, 2.30 and 2.21 respectively.  
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
8.2  The Committee was informed that in Bihar and C hhattisgarh reduction in prevalence of leprosy star ted late, due to late 
introduction of Multi Drug Therapy (MDT) for treatm ent of leprosy. D and N Haveli being an industrial area has large number 
of migratory cases from nearby States/UTs, and due to less population, a small increase in the number of recorded cases resulted 
in enormous increase in the prevalence rate. 
Action Taken  
Matter of record. 
X.   NATIONAL IODINE DEFICIENCY DISORDERS CONTROL P ROGRAMME 
Recommendations/Observations  
10.1   Deficiency of iodine can cause physical and mental retardation, cretinism, abortions, still-birth, deaf, mutism, squint 
and various types of goiter. Out of 325 districts c overing all the States and Union Territories where sample surveys were 
conducted, 263 districts have been found to be endemic, where the prevalence of iodine deficiency disorder is more than 10 per 
cent and estimated 71 million people are suffering from Iodine Deficiency Disorders. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
10.2   The budget allocation under the Programme fo r 2009-10 was Rs.40 crore which was reduced at the RE stage to 
Rs.29 crore. The Department has informed that uptill 28.2.2010, the fund utilization was to the tune of Rs.15.32 crore. On being 
asked regarding the drastic reduction in the alloca tion at RE 2009-10, the Department has informed tha t the reduced allocation 
was mainly due to review of the technical specifications of salt testing kits, and approval of Empowered Programme Committee 
(EPC) being under consideration. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
10.3  The Committee is not very happy with the tren ds of fund utilization under the programme. During 2007-08 and 2008-09 
against an allocation of Rs.25.00 crore and Rs.32.0 0 crore respectively, expenditure reported is only Rs.19.45 crore and 
Rs.17.95 crore respectively. The Committee observes  that every year there have been substantial amount  of savings over the 
allocated funds. The Committee notes with disappoin tment that this neither speaks of good financial ma nagement nor good 
programme implementation under the Programme. The C ommittee once again recommends to the Department to  devise 
measures to overcome the obstacles coming in the way of proper implementation of the programme initiatives. 
Action Taken  
  The actual allocation and expenditure during the year 2007-08 and 2008-09 under National Iodine Defi ciency Disorders 
Control Programme (NIDDCP) is as below:

CHAPTER-II 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS WHICH THE COMMITTEE DOES NOT DESIRE TO PURSUE IN 
VIEW OF THE GOVERNMENT’S REPLIES 
II.  BUDGETARY PROVISION 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.4   The Committee views with serious concern the trend of allocation of funds and utilization thereo f in some other crucial 
programmes such as National Programme for Control o f Blindness, Iodine Deficiency Disorder Control Pro gramme, 
Procurement of Drugs and Equipments under RCH, Information Education and Communication (Both RCH and Non-RCH) and 
Area Projects are some of the other programmes unde r which performance level during the Eleventh Plan so far has also been 
far from satisfactory. The Committee has been repea tedly cautioning the Department to take remedial me asures to ensure 
optimum utilization of funds year after year. Howev er, no significant improvement has been noticed so far. The case for 
Procurement of Drugs and Equipments under RCH remai ns a classic example, where, during 2008-09 Rs.300 crore were 
allocated and no expenditure was made. Again, in 20 09-10 an initial outlay of Rs.1 00 crore were made under the same head 
which was revised upwards to Rs.150 crore and no expenditure is reported to be there up till January, 2010. Approved outlay of 
Rs.1500 crore for the Eleventh Plan has thus remained unspent in spite of three years of the Plan period being over. 
Action Taken  
  Under the scheme Procurement of Drug and Equipmen ts under RCH, RE (2009-10) was Rs.150 crores and as  per the 
status of expenditure as on 31.3.2010, the funds under the scheme stand fully utilized. However, during 2008-09, no funds could 
be utilized against BE of Rs.300 crores due to non-finalisation of MOU with World Bank. As desired by the Committee, sincere 
efforts will continue to be made by the Ministry to  ensure optimum and judicious utilization of funds under each and every 
scheme. 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.5   The Committee strongly feel that poor plannin g and execution on the part of the Department regar ding the various on-
going schemes have been responsible for such a disc ouraging scenario. The Committee is well aware of t he fact that in a vast 

country like ours, with healthcare facilities espec ially in the rural areas showing a negligible prese nce, need for additional fund 
will also be there. The Committee has also been str ongly advocating the case for enhanced allocation o f funds for NRHM but 
the trend of under-utilization/unutilisation weakens the justification for higher provision of funds. The Committee is also aware 
of the fact that Union Government alone cannot achieve the targeted goals. It has to carry out the implementation with the active 
support of State Governments. The Committee, theref ore, once again recommends the Department to make i ts monitoring 
mechanism more effective so that such large amount of funds do not lapse/remain unutilized. The Commit tee also suggests the 
Department to undertake a comprehensive self-assess ment study on the means and methods used to improve  financial 
accountability under the Mission so far, and based on the findings, to devise a mechanism whereby funds utilization for the rest 
of the Mission period could be in a better state. T aking note of significant and persistent shortfall in utilization of the budgeted 
funds, the Committee would like the Department to e xercise greater prudence in their budget formulatio n and make their 
estimates only after proper assessment and planning. 
Action Taken  
  The Ministry has put into place a comprehensive, multi projected strategy for regular monitoring, co ncurrent evaluation 
and local feedback with respect to various components of the schemes. Monthly review meeting on expenditure are being taken 
by Secretary. (H&FW) with senior level officers. Pe riodic review meeting are also taken by Secretary w ith State Health 
Secretaries and Mission Directors of the States. 
  The Ministry has taken all efforts to utilize the  allocated funds. A Financial Management Group (FMG) has been set up in 
the Ministry to oversee the release of funds, monit oring of Utilisation Certificates and Audit Reports . The increased focus on 
accurate and timely reporting of SOEs and UCs has substantially improved the status of outstanding funds with the States. Since 
the number of financial transactions in the sector is expected to increase substantially in the coming  years, the NRHM has 
envisaged augmentation of accounting manpower at th e level of the PHCs in all States. The State Progra mme Management 
Units (PMUs) have also been advised to constitute F MGs within the PMUs to facilitate accurate and time ly reporting of health 
sector allocations. With the enhanced flexibility and institutional structures strengthened at the ground level, fuller utilization of 
allocated outlay is expected in future 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.6   NRHM is targeted to provide accessible, affor dable and equitable health care services for people  living in rural areas of 
the country with focus on under-served population a nd marginalized groups. Therefore, it is the consid ered view of the 
Committee that under-funding should not be allowed to come in the way of delivery of essential and qua lity health care to the 
most vulnerable sections of our society. The Committee, therefore, recommends that additional funds be allocated under NRHM 
wherever required 
Action Taken  
  The Department would like to thank the Committee for recommending more funds for NRHM. The framework  for 
implementation had envisaged an increase of 40% eve ry year. Unfortunately, the increase is now in the range of 10-20% every 
year. With increased absorptive capacity in the Sta tes, the allocation is inadequate to meet the chall enge of setting up an 
effective public health system. There are constrain ts on absorptive capacity with regard to physical i nfrastructure development 
in States like Orissa, Bihar and Jharkhand which ar e being addressed through state specific system int erventions. The Ministry 
will make even greater efforts to increase the absorptive capacity in the coming years  
Recommendations/Observations  
2.7   The twin problems of pending Utilization Cert ificates (UCs) and Unspent Balances under various 
programmes/components of NRHM have been engaging th e attention of the Committee since long. The Commit tee finds that 
the year 2009-10 was also no different. The Committ ee was informed that as on 15th April, 2009, there were 2083 UCs 
amounting to Rs. 7785.90 crore outstanding in respect of grants released upto March, 2008. This pendency must have increased 
manifold by the end of March, 2010. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 

Recommendations/Observations  
2.8   On being asked specifically about the measure s taken by the Department to liquidate pending Util ization Certificates, the 
Department has informed that as per the provision of the General Financial Rules it is necessary that in case of recurring grants, 
funds for the same purpose beyond 75% of the next y ear’s provision will not be released unless audited  Utilisation Certificate 
for the grants released in the previous year is rec eived. Further, for non-recurring grants, no fresh releases are being authorized 
unless audited Utilisation Certificate for grants released in the year prior to the last year ( i.e. , with a ‘grace period’ of one year) 
has been received. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.10  The Committee observes that 2083 number of pe nding UCs amounting to Rs.7785.00 crore is too large to be comfortable 
with. The Committee notes that the biggest defaulte rs in terms of amount involved in pending UCs are U ttar Pradesh 
(Rs.1097.39 crore), Karnataka (Rs. 596.08 crore), B ihar (Rs.537.74 crore) and Assam (Rs.437.11 crore) and unreconciled 
amount is to the tune of Rs.707.40 crore involved i n 31 UCs. The Committee is also astonished to find that the pendency of 
UCs has been continuing year after year, especially in case of the High Focus States, EAG States and NE Region, although well 
over two-thirds of the time period of NRHM(2005- 10 ) is over. What is more astonishing is that Union T erritories like Delhi, 
Chandigarh, Puducherry and A&N Islands also figure in the list. 
Action Taken  
  The Ministry is happy to report that as a result of relentless efforts made in this regard, out of p ending UCs amounting to 
Rs.7785 crores, UCs worth over Rs.5000 crores were settled during the year 2009-l0.The UCs of 2009-10 would be formally 
settled once the audit reports of States/UTs are received in August-September, 2010. That would substantially liquidate unspent 
balances and pendency of Utilization Certificates. 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.11  The Committee would like to point out that me re advisories/ DOs to States for liquidation of pending UCs is not a tenable 
solution for expeditious liquidation of pending UCs. The Committee is of the view that monitoring of p ending UCs needs to be 
further strengthened. The Committee also recommends  that a senior officer be assigned the task of lais ing with the States and 
monitor the progress in this regard on a quarterly basis. The Committee would like to be apprised of the action taken to revamp 
the monitoring of pending UCs. The Committee is als o of the view that the Department take up the matte r with the States 
showing maximum pendency of UCs at the highest level impressing upon them the imperative of furnishing the UCs timely and 
exercising fiscal discipline.  
Action Taken  
  In order to streamline the monitoring of UCs, a n ew software has been evolved in Pay and Accounts Of fice to keep track 
of all UCs. It is aimed to give reports periodicall y to respective Divisions to enable them to take up  the matter with the States 
showing low utilization so that necessary steps can be taken to improve the situation. 
Recommendations/Observations  
2.12  The Committee observes that availability of u nspent balances continues to remain on the high sid e, both under Mission 
Flexible Pool and RCH Flexible Pool, the two major components under NRHM. Opening balance as on 01.04. 2008 with 
different State Governments/DTs under Mission Flexi ble Pool was Rs.4150.07 crore, while funds provided  during the year 
2008-09 were Rs.2597.44 crore. Against the total fu nds amounting to Rs.6747.51 crore with the State, u tilization figures are 
only Rs.3254.29 crore, less than half of the total availability of funds. The Committee finds that final position as on 01.04.2009 
was no different from the previous year as opening balance of Rs.3493.22 crore was again lying with th e State Governments.

Action Taken  
  As explained earlier, the utilization of funds un der the flexible pools has substantially increased every year. A 
comparative chart is given in the Annexure-III.  
Recommendations/Observations  
2.14   The Committee is surprised to note that unde r RCH Flexible Pool also Opening Balance amounting to Rs.1157.46 
crore was available as on 01.04.2008 with different  States. Assam with availability of Rs.126.67 crore , Maharashtra with 
Rs.209.69 crore, Tamil Nadu with Rs.97.73 crore, U. P. with Rs.192.33 crore and West Bengal with Rs.101 .59 crore can be 
considered slow performing States. Not only this, S tates like Goa, Meghalaya and Tripura failed to uti lise even the opening 
balance during the course of the year. Percentage o f utilization of total allocated funds (opening bal ance + Release) was very 
low in respect of A&N Islands (44%), Assam (53%), G oa(23%), Haryana (52%), Himachal Pradesh (53%), J&K  (40%), 
Manipur (54%), Meghalaya (26%), Nagaland (45%), Tam il Nadu (40%), Tripura (33%) and West Bengal (40%).  The 
Committee is very disappointed with such a utilization status, specially because under RCH Flexible Pool, States/ UTs have the 
flexibility to plan on a need basis without being b ound by rigid normative prescriptions. The Committe e, therefore, feels that 
with more than two-third of the mission period alre ady being over, it is high time that the Department  identifies the problem 
areas in the implementation of various components o f RCH Flexible Pool and takes measures for resolvin g the same. A 
beginning can be made by approaching the states lagging behind on a priority basis. 

Action Taken  
  Considering the status of low utilization of fund s by the States, an exercise was undertaken and joi nt teams of the 
MoHFW and National Health System Resource Centre (N HSRC) were sent to various States to identify the r easons and to 
address the same in the State PIPs for 2010-11. In order to bring about improvement in utilization of funds, the Financial 
Monitoring Reports (FMRs) are being regularly revie wed and reasons for low utilization are being commu nicated to the States 
on a quarterly basis. In addition to this, regular financial management reviews are also being underta ken by the Financial 
Management Group (FMG) and appropriate recommendati ons being made to the States. The Statutory Audits and Concurrent 
Audits of the State and District Health Societies a nd all divisions of Disease Control Programmes are being emphasized upon 
and followed up so as to highlight usage patterns, reasons for low utilization and suggest remedial measures. 
  The unspent balances as on 1.4.2008 year have bee n reduced from Rs.192.33 crores to Rs.93.86 crores in respect of Uttar 
Pradesh, from Rs.209.69 crores to Rs.118.40 crores in respect of Maharashtra, at the close of the fina ncial year. The remaining 
three slow performing States referred to above have reported increased expenditure in the financial year 2008-09 as compared to 
the previous financial year: Assam from Rs.105.24 c rores to Rs.194.74 crores; Tamil Nadu from Rs.77.03  crores to Rs.97.03 
crores; and West Bengal from Rs.78.67 crores to Rs.130.85 crores. 
  The percentage utilisation of allocated funds up to the year 2008-09 has increased as compared to th e previous financial 
year i.e.  from 44% to 77.13% in A & N Islands, from 53% to 6 8.34% in Assam, from 23% to 42.51% in Goa, from 52%  to 
72.55 % in Haryana, from 53% to 68.07% in Himachal Pradesh, from 40% to 60.66% in J & K, from 26% to 9 5.64% in 
Mizoram, from 45% to 62.93% in Nagaland, from 40% t o 63.32% in Tamil Nadu, from 33% to 45.24% in Tripu ra and from 
40% to 64.78% in West Bengal. 
  The amount of unspent balances with the States as  on 01.04.2008 was Rs.1157.46 crores which has been  reduced to 
Rs.1051.66 crores as on 1.4.2009. 
III.  INFRASTRUCTURE STRENGTHENING 
Healthcare Units  
Recommendations/Observations  
3.2   The Committee while taking note of progress m ade in strengthening the primary health infrastruct ure after the launch of 
NRHM would like to point out that considerable gap still continues to persist. In this connection, Com mittee’s attention has 
been drawn by Performance Audit of NRHM by CAG (Rep ort No.8 of 2009-10). There was a shortfall of 4398 7 Sub-Centres 
(27%) in 22 States/ UTs, 8613 PHCs (31 %) in 21 Sta tes/ UTs and 4200 CHCs (55%) in 23 States/UTs which  were required to 
be created during the NRHM period (2005-12). Target of creation of 30% of new infrastructure by 2007, i.e. 13196 sub-centres, 
2585 PHCs and 1261 CHCs could not be fulfilled. Acc ording to the CAG Report, as many as 14 States/UTs failed to take any 
initiative for bridging the gap. Out of the target of 43987 sub-centres, 8613 PHCs and 4200 CHCs only 2562 Sub-Centres, 685 
PHCs and 138 CHCs could be set-up. The Committee only hopes that 935 Sub--Centres, 178 PHCs and 67 CHCs reported to be 
under construction during CAG Audit must have been completed and become fully functional by now. The C ommittee would 
like to point out that even as per the target of 20 07, there was a shortfall of 9749 sub-centres, also  1722 PHCs and 1056 CHCs. 
Against this backdrop, bridging the gap of 43987 su b-centres, 8613 PHCs and 4200 CHCs by the end of mi ssion period i.e. 
2012 seems to be an uphill task. The Committee woul d like to point out that the targets set up under N RHM were as per the 
population norms of 2001. The shortfall of infrastructure is bound to increase manifold once 2011 census is over and population 
norms revise. The Committee has also taken note of the fact that only very few states like Assam, Chha ttisgarh, Rajasthan, 
Uttarakhand and Lakshadweep had made an attempt to achieve the target of 2007, while the remaining Sta tes reported nil 
achievement. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Department has to pursue vigorously with all the non-performing 
States, otherwise even the basic objective of NRHM of having the minimum required infrastructure would  also remain 
unfulfilled. 
Action Taken  

  While considerable progress has been made from 20 05 to 2009, there is a long way to go in meeting the full requirements 
of infrastructure. A comparison of Primary Health Care Infrastructure and Human Resources in HSCs, PHCs and CHCs is given 
in the Annexure-IV .

2005) at Sub-Centres/PHCs presently. From a detaile d analysis of the information furnished by the Depa rtment the Committee

Recommendations/Observations  
3.10  Not only this, with regard to a number of cru cial States/UTs like Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, J&K,  Jharkhand, Tripura 
and A&N Islands, information about even sanctioned posts was not available with the Centre. These were also States where in-
position ANMs exceeded their required strength. The  Committee also notes that 46,690 ANMs have been re cruited on contract 
basis by the States across the country. The Committ ee would like to point out that the filling of vaca ncy of ANMs at Sub-
Centres and PHCs is a thrust area under NRHM. Howev er, the clarification given by the Department that limited capacity at 
ANM schools poses problem in availability of ANMs d epicts another glaring shortcoming in the provision  of healthcare 
facilities in rural areas. The Committee is not rea dy to accept the contention of the Department that since the training of ANM 
requires 18 months, it will take some time in availability of ANMs and filling up of the vacant ANM posts at various levels. The 
fact remains that the key functionary, i.e.  ANM, is not there at all the health centres indica tes that large number of targeted 
beneficiaries still continue to be deprived of the very basic healthcare facilities. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record.

Recommendations/Observations  
3.21  The Committee feels that for effective delive ry of public health services, it is essential that service personnel are present 
continuously in their place of postings. However, w ith more and more number of contractual manpower be ing recruited under 
NRHM, the Committee fears that the accountability m echanism under the programme would get diluted, the reby affecting the 
delivery of quality services to the masses. The Com mittee, accordingly, recommends to the Department t hat an All India level 
drive for filling up of the vacant posts, first aga inst the sanctioned strength and thereafter against the required strength needs to 
be undertaken in a mission mode so that by the end of NRHM significant improvement is more than evident. 
Action Taken  
  NRHM has promoted recruitment of contractual staf f because health is a State subject and it is reall y for the State 
Governments to decide on a long term sustainability  of a special human resource. Also NRHM is about he alth sector reforms 
and it was felt that in order to ensure health work ers in remote areas, institution specific recruitme nts have to be made. These 
can only be done on contract for a specific facilit y. NRHM’s thrust has been on service guarantee and not on employment 
guarantee. 
IV.  MISSION FLEXIBLE POOL 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.1   Mission Flexible Pool provides for activities  like selection and training of ASHAs, upgradation of CHCs and PHCs to 
First Referral Units and Indian Public Health Stand ards (IPHS), constitution of Rogi Kalyan Samitis an d District Hospital 

Management Committees, Mobile Medical Units, untied  funds for Sub-Centres etc. States have the flexibi lity of utilizing the 
funds as per their priorities for the approved acti vities. The Committee notes that maximum expenditur e (31 %) is towards 
infrastructure upgradation, followed by Untied Funds (24%) and State Innovations (9%). 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.2   The Committee has been informed that the tota l approved outlay under Mission Flexible Pool for t he Eleventh Five Year 
Plan is Rs.33181.07 crore and the expenditure figur es for 2007-08, 2008-09 and 2009-10 (upto January 2 010) are Rs.3151.64 
crore, Rs.2599.13 crore and Rs.2479.17 crore respectively, totaling Rs.8229.94 crore for the three years taken together. Thus an 
amount of Rs.24951.13 crore (approx.) is still left  to be utilized during the remaining two financial years of the Eleventh Plan. 
However, against the projected demand of Rs.8000.00 crore, plan allocation of Rs.3569.70 crore, has been made in 2010-11. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.3   The Committee observes that allocation of Rs. 3567.27 crore for 2009-10 was reduced to Rs.3276.17  crore at the RE 
stage. This cut was imposed by the Ministry of Finance on account of slow pace of expenditure. The Committee is concerned to 
note that as on 31st January, 2010, expenditure reported was only Rs.2479.17 crore. Position was no better during 2008-09 with 
States like U.P., Orissa, Madhya Pradesh, Himacha1 Pradesh could utilize less than 50% while Bihar and  Chhattisgarh were 
able to spend only 13% and 17% respectively. Very l ow use of untied funds provided under the Mission F lexible Pool in Bihar 
and delayed transfer of funds to the districts in C hhattisgarh have been cited as the main reason for unsatisfactory performance 
in the two States. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.4   The Committee is anguished to note the huge s hortfall in utilization of the budgeted funds and a lso the substantial 
variation between the projected demand for funds an d the sanctioned provisions. The Committee observes  that at a time when 
equitable access to health facilities across the so cial and geographical expanse of the country is the  declared policy of the 
Government, such inefficiencies in utilization of f unds can prove to be a potent deterrent. The Commit tee would like the 
Department to impress upon all concerned including the State Governments the imperative need to review  their existing 
mechanism for assessment of requirement of funds an d take corrective action to overcome the deficienci es in the existing 
system so that the possibility of surrendering unspent/under-utilized funds is obviated.  
Action Taken  
  The utilization of funds as pointed out earlier h as increased considerably under the Mission Flexibl e Pool. In terms of 
release of funds the Mission Flexible Pool has alwa ys utilized more than 99% of the allocated resource s in the last five years. 
Utilization in the States did take a little while but even that is now improved considerably. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.9   As per the mandate of Implementation Framewor k of NRHM, all Integrated District Health Action Pl ans (IDHAP) were 
to be finalised by the end of 2008. On being asked regarding the latest State-wise progress made so fa r, the Department has 
informed that though there was a delay initially, n ow States/UTs have shown remarkable improvement for  preparation of 
IDHAP. As on date, 617 districts of the country have already finalised IDHAPs, and teams from NHSRC were sent to backward 
districts to facilitate decentralised planning. 

Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.10   The Committee, however, notes that only 22 S tates/UTs have furnished IDHAPs fully, while, even by the end of 
March 2010, as many as 12 States/UTs are yet to fur nish the IDHAPs 100 per cent. Goa, D&N Haveli and L akshadweep have 
not been able to submit a single IDHAP, while Rajas than has been able to furnish only 26 out of the 33  IDHAPs. This is the 
position when timeline to complete the submission of IDHAPs by the States/UTs was by the end of 2008. The Committee fails 
to understand what could be the possible reasons fo r such inordinate delays in the submission of IDHAP s. The Committee 
would like to emphasise that Integrated District He alth Action Plans are the very basis for improving the availability of health 
care facilities in a district. In the absence of a concrete plan of action drawn by the districts, pro per health care facilities for 
people residing in such districts cannot be develop ed optimally. The Committee therefore, impresses up on the Department to 
vigorously pursue this issue with concerned States/UTs to furnish the same as early as possible.  
Action Taken  
  All districts have now been doing the Districts P lans and teams from the Central Government have bee n visiting the 
districts to facilitate the process. 
Recommendations/Observations  
4.13  Grants to State Training Institutions  
  The Committee notes that out of this allocation, major funding of Rs. 520.48 crore is meant for basi c training for ANMs/ 
LHVs and Rs. 56.09 crore for basic training for MPW s (Male). The Committee notes that out of Rs. 520.4 8 crore kept for 
training of ANMs/ LHVs, only Rs. 218.04 crore have been released during the first three years of the E leventh Plan. With Rs. 
85.18 crore proposed for 2010-11, more than Rs. 200 .00 crore would be left for the last year of the pl an period. Not only this, 
during 2008-09 and 2009-10, the allocated funds have remained under-utilised. The Committee finds that funds meant for basic 
training for MPW s (Male) also could not be put to optimum use during the Eleventh Plan period so far. 
Action Taken  
  The eleventh Plan outlay is an indicative outlay,  which will be available for the Department on annu al basis depending 
upon the utilization of funds and it is the jurisdi ction of the Planning Commission to decide the Plan  Outlay for the Ministries/ 
Departments based on the overall resources available and priorities decided by the Planning Commission. 
  The year-wise Approved Outlay (BE) and Expenditur e under the schemes namely Basic Training of ANM/LH V s and 
Basic Training of MPW Workers (Male) during 2007-08 to 2009-10 and BE for 2010-11 is given in the Annexure- V  
  Under the scheme, funds are released to the State s for salary of the staff, rent for school and host el, stipend for trainees, 
educational aids and training material, hiring for bus and contingency. The utilization of funds under  the above schemes were 
less during 2008-09 and 2009-10 due to unspent bala nces of the previous years lying with the States. T he approved outlay for 
the above schemes has been increased in 2010-11 aga inst the approved outlay in 2009-10 to pay the arre ars of the staffs posted 
at Training Schools, on account of implementation of 6th Pay Commission Report by the States.  
Recommendations/Observations  
4.14   The Committee is not aware about the reasons  for lack of interest on the part of State Governme nts for creating/ 
strengthening the training schools meant for key He alth functionaries with the support of Central Fund s. The Committee 
strongly feels that it is high time for the Departm ent to make an assessment of this crucial area in c onsultation with all the 
States/ UTs and bring about all round improvement. One must not forget that a fully trained and qualif ied health worker alone 
can handle his job responsibilities effectively. 
Action Taken  

  Since the launch of NRHM, many ANM/LHVTCs, which were closed in some of the States, have been reopened. In some 
of the ANM Training Centres, the admission capacity  of students increased from 20 to 40 candidates and  in others from 40 to 
60 candidates per annum. For example, the State of Bihar has proposed establishment of 10 new ANMTCs a nd also 
strengthening of 21 ANMTCs in PIP 2010-11. Chhattis garh has also proposed to strengthen the ANMTC of D hentari District 
under Tribal development. They have also proposed t raining to Mitanin for ANM and nursing for their ca reer progression. In 
Rajasthan, five new ANMTCs have been opened under N RHM and they are proposing to open one more ANMTC i n the new 
district in PIP 2010-11. They have also proposed in PIP 2010-11 to enhance the seats of ANM trainees from 60 to 90. 
  Under the scheme of Upgradation/Strengthening of Nursing Services, there is a provision to open new ANM Training 
Schools and about 132 ANM Training Schools are proposed to be opened in the different districts of the States. 
  As regards LHVTCs many States are reopening the L HVTCs also. Senior ANM with five years of experienc e. is given 
six months promotional training to become LHV /Heal th Assistant (Female). Health Assistants (Female)/L HV provides 
supportive supervision and technical guidance to th e ANMs in sub-centres. About 34 LHVTCs are function al, including 4 in 
UP. 
  Regarding MPHW(M) Training Schools, in UP 5000 MH W(M) are being proposed to be trained. 2400 FHW are  also 
being proposed to be trained in 40 ANMTCs and it is  already approved in PIP 2010-11. It may be mention ed that 49 
MPHW(M) Training Centres are functional in the coun try, each with an admission capacity of about 60 tr ainees per annum. In 
other States, where these schools were not function al, they have started functioning. For example, in Chhattisgarh three 
MPHW(M)TCs started functioning. In many States (lik e Maharashtra, Andhra Pradesh, Karnataka and Jharkh and) MPHW(M) 
training are being imparted by the Health and Family Welfare Training Centres. 
  In the 11th meeting of EPC of NRHM on 10th May 20 10, it was proposed to have provision Health Worker (Male) in 235 
high focus districts. 
V.  RCH FLEXIBLE POOL 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.1   The main objective of RCH Flexible Pool is to  bring about the change in the three critical healt h indicators: Infant 
Mortality Rate (IMR), Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR ) and Total Fertility Rate (TFR) consistent with th e goal of National 
Population Policy 2002, the Tenth and Eleventh Plans and Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). The targets to be achieved 
by the year 2012 are to bring down IMR to less than  30, MMR to less than 100 and TFR to 2.1. Against t his, the achievement 
levels as per latest available studies are: IMR 53 (SRS 2008), MMR 254 (SRS 2004-06) & TFR 2.6 (SRS 20 08). One of the 
most important strategies adopted under the Program me is the freedom given to the States to prepare th eir Annual State and 
District level Project Implementation Plan (PIPs) after assessing their need- based requirements. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.2   Secretary, Health, during her presentation be fore the Committee, highlighted the main strategies  for Maternal Health and 
stated that the main factors for high rate of MMR could be broadly classified as delay in decision-making, delay in reaching the 
hospital and delay in initiating appropriate care. Delay in decision-making takes place mainly due to lack of awareness and 
financial constraints. The Committee was informed t hat to tackle these basic constraints, a number of significant interventions 
have been made so far. Awareness and counseling thr ough ASHAs/ANMs/AWWs, availability of financial ass istance through 
Janani Suraksha Yojana for institutional delivery, and skilled providers for domiciliary birth were ci ted as the main 
interventions. The other problem area coming in the  way of checking MMR was delay in reaching the hosp ital due to lack of 
knowledge about danger signs and availability of tr ansport facilities. Here again, ASHAs/ANMs/ AWW s a re meant to do the 
counseling on danger signs. Transport facilities are being made available through JSY and referral services through Government 
ambulances/ PPP arrangements. Another hindrance not iced was lack of availability of operational facili ty for tracking 
pregnancy and its complications. The Committee was given to understand that these hindrances were bein g tackled by 

upgradation of infrastructure at the district level and multi-skilling of Doctors to overcome shortage of critical specialists. As on 
date, 1024 doctors have been trained in anesthesia skills, 543 doctors trained in obstetric skills and  37,162 Staff nurses/ LHVs/ 
ANMs trained as skilled birth attendants. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.3   The Committee was also apprised about the mai n factors found responsible for high rate of IMR, w hich were severe 
infections (36% deaths), pre-term birth (25% deaths ), birth asphyxia (23% deaths) and neo-natal tetanu s (4% deaths). Steps 
taken so far for checking this high incidence of ne o-natal deaths were Integrated Management of New Bo rn and Childhood 
Illness, Basic New Born Care and Navjat Shishu Suraksha Karyakaran etc. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.4   Allocation for RCH Flexible Pool for 2009-10 was Rs.3545.00 crore and the actual expenditure rep orted upto January, 
2010 was Rs.2641.84 crore. The Committee observes t hat there has been optimum utilization of allocated  funds. Against a 
provision of Rs. 1872.00 crore and Rs.3126.04 crore  in 2007-08 and 2008-09, utilization reported was R s.1999.25 crore and 
Rs.3077.16 crore respectively. Committee’s attentio n has, however, been drawn by State-wise utilizatio n status which depicts 
wide ranging disparities, that too in NE State specially as indicated below:

Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations
