year were higher than the proposed allocation. Whil e trying to understand the reasons therefor, the Co mmittee came to know 
that all the States/UTs were having unspent balance s at the beginning of the year. As per the details made available to the 
Committee, as on 01.04.2008, opening balance amount ing to Rs.1157.46 crore was available with Assam (R s.126.67 crore), 
Maharashtra (Rs.209.69 crore), Orissa (Rs.60.99 crore), Tamil Nadu (Rs.97.73 crore), D.P. (Rs.192.33 c rore) and West Bengal 
(Rs.101.59 crore) reporting the maximum. 
Action Taken  
  As regards devising alternative strategies so tha t funds allocated are utilized optimally so that th e poor and weaker 
sections of the society are not bereft of the benef its of various on-going health care initiatives, ac tion is being taken on the 
following key areas identified for priority action during 2010:-

/circle6 Action Plan for streamlining of procurement and lo gistics, 
/circle6 Action Plan for operationalising HMIS up to facili ty level 
/circle6 Capacity Development of all Institutions crafted u nder NRHM 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.6  This clearly shows that States have generally failed to take the full benefit of various Central interventions inspite of their 
having the discretion to spend money as per their r equirements. The Committee, therefore, strongly fee ls that if despite the 
availability of funds the States are unable to util ize them, and thus is a cause for a thorough review  of strategies. The low 

utilization of funds has seriously hampered Governm ent’s initiatives for bringing down IMR, MMR and TF R. Seriously 
concerned with this state of affairs, the Committee  recommends that the Department, in consultation wi th the States/UTs, 
should devise alternative strategies so that the fu nds allocated are utilized optimally so that the po or and weaker sections of the 
society are not bereft of the benefits of various on-going health care initiatives. 
Action Taken  
/circle6 Higher utilization of financial resources under NRHM, and 
/circle6 Plan for human resources for health including ince ntives for staff being posted in difficult, most difficult and 
inaccessible areas. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.7   On being asked as to whether any assessment h as been made so far about the main factors responsi ble for some of the 
High Focus States/NE States and Hill States of Hima chal Pradesh and J&K failing to avail of maximum fa cilities under RCH 
Flexi pool, the Department has highlighted the following main constraints:- 
– Shortage of Human resources in the relevant State s 
– Difficult geographic conditions (NE States, J&K, Uttarakhand, HP) 
– Low absorption capacity 
– Absenteeism 
– Tenures of key officers like, Programme Officers an d Administrators are not assured, which affects pro gramme 
ownership and continuity of interventions 
Action Taken

(PIPs). 
– Hiring health staff on contractual basis and local people 
– Providing hardship allowance to health staff  
– Capacity building of the State and district programme officers in decentralized planning and financial matters.

  Matter of record.

flagged off by the Department, and asking for funds to overcome them. 
Action Taken

prescribed in consultation with the States/UTs to b e achieved by them during 2010-11 and specifically included in the 
administrative approvals being issued for the State  Programme Implementation Plans. The details are gi ven in the Annexure- 
VIII . 
ROUTINE  IMMUNIZATION  

Recommendations/Observations  
5.10   A provision of Rs.2457.16 crore has been mad e for Routine Immunization during the Eleventh Plan . The 
Committee observes that funds allocated during the first three years have remained under-utilised with  only less than 50 per 
cent being spent so far. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.11   The Committee was informed that against the budget allocation of Rs.466.30 crore during the yea r 2008-09, a sum 
of Rs.159.96 crore was utilized. Out of this Rs.145 .08 crore was utilized for procurement of vaccines including Hepatitis B. 
Further, the saving was mainly under the sub-head ‘Vaccine for Hepatitis B/Pentavalent Vaccine’ for which a fund ofRs.205.30 
crore was allocated out of which Rs.21.16 crore cou ld only be utilized as the scheme regarding introdu ction of Pentavalent 
Vaccine was deferred for technical re-examination o f the issue. Out of the budget provision of Rs.86.0  crore under Needle 
syringes and Rs. 47.0 crore under cold chain equipm ent, a sum of Rs.13 .09 crore and Rs.1. 79 crore wa s utilized respectively, 
due to non-finalization/less procurement of these items. 
Action Taken.  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.12   Allocation in 2010-11 has been increased to Rs.417 crore against an allocation of Rs.388.21 cro re during 2009-10. 
The Committee was given to understand that the enha nced allocation of RS.28.79 crores has been propose d for procurement of 
cold chain equipments and Hepatitis B/ Pentavalent vaccines. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.13   The Committee is constrained to observe that  this is yet another case of inept handling of budg eted funds by the 
Department. The Committee finds to its dismay that despite the efficacy and relevance of Pentavalent V accine being in 
question, a substantive amount of Rs.205.30 crore was allocated for the same out of which Rs.184.14 cr ore remained unutilised 
and had to be surrendered. The Committee would like  the Department to exercise greater caution in future and not allow repeat 
of such fiscal imprudence. 
Action Taken  
  During 2008-09, a sum of Rs.205.30 crores was all ocated for pentavalent vaccine introduction. However, scheme for the 
introduction of the pentavalent vaccine was deferred for technical re-examination. During the year 200 9-10, funds to the extent 
of Rs.50 crore were earmarked under the sub head “V accine for Hepatitis B/Pentavalent Vaccine”. In the current financial year 
i.e 2010-11 only a token provision has been kept fo r pentavalent vaccine as requisite administrative a pprovals for its 
introduction are yet to be obtained. Further, the i ssue of introduction of pentavalent vaccine has bec ome sub-judice. The 
observation of the Committee has been noted for compliance.

PULSE  POLIO  PROGRAMME  
Recommendations/Observations  
5.19   Under Pulse Polio Programme,  Rs.1150.94 crore were allocated in BE 2009-10 whic h was revised upwards to 
Rs.1191.31 crore at the RE stage, with full utilization by the end of the year. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  

5.20   The Committee was informed that the increase d allocation during 2009-10 at RE stage was based on the number of 
rounds i.e. two National Immunization Day (NIDs), 7  Sub-National Immunization Days (SNIDs) and 3 Mop-U p. As compared 
to 2009-10, a reduced provision of Rs. 1017.08 cror e has been made for 2010-11. It has been clarified that during 2010-11, 
based on the epidemiological picture of polio infec tion being confined to western Uttar Pradesh and Ko si region of Bihar, the 
area of operation has reduced and hence the reduced allocation would not affect the on-going activities. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.21  The Committee has been informed that currentl y type 3 & type 1 virus was in circulation, limited  mainly to 107 high risk 
blocks of Uttar Pradesh and Bihar. High density pop ulations, poor hygiene practices, nearly non-existe nt sanitation 
infrastructure, compromised drinking water supply, inadequate health and routine immunization services , high malnutrition 
rates, and poverty have been cited as the main factors responsible for such a scenario. A multi-pronged strategy is being planned 
which includes cross-cutting activities to minimize  the factors facilitating poliovirus transmission i n these 107 blocks while at 
the same time ensuring the highest possible quality of polio immunization activities. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.22   As a special intervention, the new-born trac king is being done in U.P. and Bihar for coverage d uring pulse polio 
round upto the age of one year, (now been extended to all the States). It has also stated that althoug h in 2009, a total of 602 
polio cases were reported compared to 305 cases in 2008 from Uttar Pradesh, it may be noted that only 34 cases were due to 
more virulent Wild Polio Virus (WPV)-l type as against 62 cases of WPV-1 in 2008. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.23   The Committee is happy to note that the Depa rtment has been diligently pressing forward with various measures to 
contain the spread of WPV-1 and WPV-3 types, especi ally entrenched in Bihar and Uttar Pradesh. The Com mittee hopes that 
the introduction of the bivalent vaccine (P1& P3) d uring 2010 would go a long way in eradicating polio  from the country. 
Though the Department has cited that the absolute numbers of polio cases have been reducing over the years, given the massive 
scale on which the programme is being implemented with NIDs, SNIDs and mop-up rounds reported polio cases have shown no 
significant improvement during the past three years. The Committee would like to reckon here that as per the findings of DLHS 
III (2007-08) survey, the all India figures of polio immunization stand at 66.2% only, with Bihar (53.1 %), UP (44.4%), Madhya 
Pradesh (55.1%), Meghalaya (46.5%), Jharkhand (64.4 %), Rajasthan (63.9%) and most of the NE States per forming less than 
the all India average. Given the above levels of PP I coverage in various States, the Committee would l ike the Department to 
explain us as to how the goal of zero transmission can be achieved at the earliest.  
Action Taken  
  The bivalent vaccine has been introduced in the P ulse Polio Programme in 2010 and has been mainly used in the high risk 
areas of UP and Bihar. The NIDs, SNIDs and mop ups are also being implemented as guided by the epidemi ology of the 
poliovirus in the country. The number of polio cases during the first 19 weeks has declined from 40 cases in 2009 to 21 cases in 
2010 for a similar period during the 2 years.  
  The immunization coverage of 66.2% assessed throu gh DLHS III and mentioned above pertains to the evaluated coverage 
of polio vaccination through routine immunization programme and not through the pulse polio immunization programme (PPI). 
The figures of 53.1 % for Bihar and 44.6% for UP also pertain to the polio vaccination coverage through routine immunization. 

  As a part of the strategy to eradicate polio, mul tiple Pulse Polio Immunization (PPI) campaigns are being conducted in the 
high risk States in addition to the routine immuniz ation programme. The overall polio vaccination cove rage in UP and Bihar 
during these campaigns is extremely high. Independe nt monitoring of the pulse Polio Programme Immuniza tion (PPI) 
conducted by WHO-NPSP monitors has demonstrated tha t the overall pulse polio coverage in UP and Bihar has been 
maintained above 95% during each of the recent campaigns. 
  The Government is making concerted efforts to fur ther improve pulse polio immunization coverage thro ughout the 
country, especially in the high risk States of UP a nd Bihar with a focus on the 107 high risk blocks a nd the migrant population 
where the risk of a continuation of poliovirus circulation is high. 
  The Government is also making efforts to improve routine immunization across the country with a spec ial focus on the 
States that have low coverage as evaluated through DLHS III. Special intensive efforts to improve rout ine immunization 
coverage are being made in the 107 blocks that are at a high risk of poliovirus circulation by improvi ng the quality of delivery 
and access to services through improvements in micr o plans, training, community mobilization & support ive supervision and 
monitoring.

5.32   The Committee observes that the goal of redu cing MMR to 100 per lakh like births by 2012 is ver y difficult to

Action Taken  
  To overcome the shortage of manpower, EmOC traini ng has been initiated. The number of medical office rs trained in 
EmOC increased from 277 in the year 2008-09 to 543 in the year 2009-10 (% increase of 96). The pace of  the training is 
proposed to be increased further and the States are advised to fully utilize the existing capacity of the training institutes. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.33   The Committee is of the view that in the abs ence of required ground-level health functionaries who have to be 
qualified, trained and semi-skilled personnel, the outreach of JSY and other specific interventions wi ll remain confined to the 
barest minimum. Number of ambitious schemes with hi gh objectives and availability of massive funding w ill not succeed in 
reaching the targeted beneficiaries. Close monitoring, not on paper but at the ground level, in the real sense alone can strengthen 
the effective implementation of all the initiatives taken so far. 
Action Taken  

  To overcome the shortage of trained manpower at t he ground level, ANMs/SNs/LHVs are being trained as  Skilled Birth 
Attendants. The total number of ANMs, LHVs and SNs trained as SBA has increased from 17,922 in the yea r 2008-09 to 
37,162 in the year 2009-10. (% increase of 107) 
  JSY has witnessed a phenomenal increase since its  inception in 2005. The number of JSY beneficiaries  has increased 
from 7.39 lakhs in the year 2005-06 to 102.77 lakhs  in the year 2009-10. The amount incurred on JSY in centive has increased 
from Rs.38.29 crores to Rs.1476.03 crores in the year 2009-10. 
  The overall monitoring mechanism in respect of JS Y has been explained in detail in the Action Taken Note against 
paragraph 5.27. However, the observations of the Committee regarding close monitoring at the ground level have been noted for 
compliance. 
VI.   NATIONAL VECTOR BORNE DISEASE CONTROL PROGRAM ME (NVBDCP) 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.1.   The National Vector Borne Disease Control Pr ogramme is a comprehensive programme for prevention  and control of 
vector borne diseases namely Malaria, Filaria, Kala -azar, Japanese Encephalitis (JE), Dengue and Chiku ngunya which is 
covered under the overall umbrella of NRHM. Central Government provides technical and financial assistance and also logistics 
support and the State Governments have to meet the other requirements and operational costs. NE States  are provided 100 per 
cent central assistance under the .programme. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.2   The general strategy for prevention and contr ol of vector borne diseases under NVBDCP is through  Disease 
Management, Integrated Vector Management  and Supportive Interventions.  The Committee was informed that for the year 
2010-2011, the Directorate of NVBDCP has projected their demands amounting to Rs.698 crore. However, allocation of Rs.418 
crore has been made considering overall allocation of Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. Out of the allocation of Rs.418.00 
crore for 2010-11, the EAP component is Rs.165.26 crore (GFATM Rs.84.87 crore and World Bank Rs.80.39 crore). 
Action Taken  
Matter of record 
MALARIA  
Recommendations/Observations  
6.5   The National Health Policy (2002) has set the  goal of reduction in mortality on account of malaria by 50 per cent by 2010. 
The Committee was informed that reported Malaria cases declined from 1.78 million in 2006 to 1.53 million in 2008 and deaths 
from Malaria declined from 1708 in 2006 to 1055 in 2008. Still it continued to be a major public healt h challenge in the North 
East States, Orissa, Jharkhahd, West Bengal, and Ch hattisgarh. In 2008, these States accounted for 65 per cent of the total 
reported Malaria cases, 85 per cent of total Pf cases and 68 per cent of total reported malaria deaths. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.6  It is also informed that though the number of reported Pf cases in the country has declined but o ut of the total malaria 
cases reported, proportion of Plasmodium falciparum  cases has increased from 44.32% in 2005 to 52.09% in 2009 (Prov.) As 
per the latest information furnished by the Departm ent, in 2009, Meghalaya (with 192 deaths), Orissa ( 192), Mizoram (119), 

Tripura (62), West Bengal (74), Maharashtra (159), Nagaland (35), and Assam (63) were leading the tabl e for malaria-related 
deaths. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.7   The Committee notes that the number of cases reported is already on the rise in many of the Stat es, especially the North-
Eastern States, where the problem is acute. Under the GFATM project all the 77 district in 7 NE States, 7 districts of Jharkhand, 
6 districts of West Bengal and 16 districts of Oris sa are covered. The Committee is not fully convince d by the Departments, 
contention that due to remote and inaccessible area s with poor access to malaria diagnosis and treatme nt facilities, NE States 
continue to remain a major challenge. The Committee  would like to state here that the programme has be en these for 
considerable period and with 100 percent central fu nding and also International assistance, these chal lenges, should have been 
significantly lessened. The Committee also finds that major interventions like introduction of Rapid diagnostic kits, for the field 
workers, introduction of specialized therapy for Pf  cases and long lasting insecticidal nets have all been made for North East 
States. However, inspite of such huge funding under the programme significant progress is not visible. The Committee can only 
conclude that supervision and monitoring, which is one of the main strategies adopted for effective co ntrol of malaria, is 
perhaps not being implemented to the desired levels, especially in NE region and Orissa. 
Action Taken  
  The programme is aware that inspite of sufficient  fund provision for NE States, the desirable impact  could not be 
achieved because of the following reasons: 
/circle6 Geo-ecological condition which includes difficult terrain and conducive climatic conditions favoring perennial 
transmission. 
/circle6 The prevalence of multiple and very efficient vect ors in the region to transmit malaria. 
/circle6 The predominant species of malaria parasite is P. falciparum which is known to lead severity and may result into 
mortality if timely effective treatment is not provided. 
/circle6 The different ethnic groups are in the region with  different treatment seeking behaviour. Gol has pro vided 
additional support as below: 
– Further the new tools like RDT, ACT, LLIN have be en introduced in the recent past and scaling up thr ough 
capacity building to use these tools is under way. 
– Under new GFATM project which is likely to commen ce shortly, in addition to State Level Consultant, 
District Vector Borne Disease Consultant and additi onal Malaria Technical Supervisors will be deployed  at 
district and sub-district level respectively. 
  For enhancing surveillance, all the NE States (ex cept Sikkim) have been provided contractual Male MP Ws by Gol to be 
deployed in the problematic districts. Contractual Laboratory Technicians under GFO ATM project have also been provided. 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.8   The Committee is of the view that shortage of  manpower is the main stumbling block in the successful implementation of 
malaria control measures. The Committee has been gi ven to understand that for strengthening supervisio n and monitoring, 
additional manpower like consultants at state level , Vector Borne Diseases Consultants at district lev el and Malaria Technical 
Supervisor at sub district level have been provided  in high endemic areas under the World Bank and GFA TM supported 
projects. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  

6.10   The fact that these functionaries play a vit al role in disease detection and surveillance canno t be set aside. By not 
appointing these functionaries, the States leave a large gap in the field-level disease surveillance and treatment. The Committee 
can only conclude from the above that it would be i mpractical to expect noticeable improvements in mal aria control with key 
functionaries missing, that too, in the malaria end emic States. Hence, the Committee advises the Depar tment that, apart from 
allocating sufficient funds to these States/UTs, it  should also pursue with State Governments to fill up the vacancies without 
delay so that the targets set to control malaria co uld be met within a realistic time- frame. The Comm ittee would like the 
Department to furnish details of various measures adopted by the States Governments in the past year to fill up these vacancies.  
Action Taken  
  The recruitment status of manpower allocated unde r the programme (including project) are being monit ored regularly. 
During review meetings, these issues are flagged with the states and action taken on this issue are followed. As a result, most of 
contractual human resource provided by GOI has been  filled up. The States have been requested to exped ite to fill remaining 
posts including contractual MPWs during 2010-11.

during 2009 as compared to 2008. In order to streng then the clinical prowess of the physicians, Dte. o f NVBDCP developed 
revised guidelines on AES/JE treatment in consultat ion with experts on 4.8.2009 and circulated these t o States. Based on the 
revised guidelines, one day orientation course at H yderabad (AP) and Gorakhpur (UP) was organized by D te. of NVBDCP for 
clinicians during 2009. The JE endemic States have been time and again advised to cover children between 18-24 months under 
Routine Immunization, however, coverage of most of the States is reported to be between (30-40%). With  regard to special JE 
campaign in 7 most JE sensitive districts in Easter n Uttar Pradesh, a special campaign is being launch ed w.e.f.  31st May 2010 
which will last for 2 weeks. For carrying out effec tive IEC campaign, GOI allocated 45 and 54 lakhs to  JE endemic States 
during 2008-09 and 2009-10 respectively. Since Uttar Pradesh is contributing over 80% of AES cases and deaths, an amount of 
Rs.277.26 lakhs and Rs.141.45 lakhs respectively has been earmarked for Lab strengthening and IEC activities during 2010-11. 
Recommendations/Observations  
6.20   The Committee was also informed that apart f rom establishing field unit of NIV, Pune at BRD Med ical College, 
Gorakhpur for detecting and isolating non-JE viruse s, the management of AES/JE cases has been accorded  top priority. 

Prevention and control guidelines have already been  circulated to all the affected districts in the St ate for initiating control 
measures against entero-viruses. The diagnostic fac ilities have been strengthened at 50 sentinel and 1 3 Apex Referral 
Laboratories including 15 sentinel sites established in Uttar Pradesh. These have been supplied with d iagnostic kits free of cost 
from National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune. One Vector Borne Disease Surveillance Unit (VBDSU) and one JE sub-office 
was established at BRD Medical College, Gorakhpur, Uttar Pradesh. Lastly, for establishing a Physical,  Medicine & 
Rehabilitation (PMR) department at BRD Medical Coll ege for treating physical disabilities due to AES/J E, MOU has been 
submitted to the State of Uttar Pradesh. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations

been initiated by the Central Government. It can on ly hope that the situation would improve this year during the outbreak 
season. The Committee hopes that the manpower required to make the sentinel sites, referral labs operational and the Diagnostic 
kits provided free of cost by National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune, are in position.  
Action Taken  
  Dte. of NVBDCP organised two days workshop for St ate Programme Officers and Laboratory personnel dur ing Dec, 09 
for effective coordination and improved surveillance and diagnosis. It was attended by 53 national and international participants 
with financial support from WHO. On account of the funds provided by Dte. of NVBDCP, State of Assam has organised hands 
on training of laboratory personnel of the identifi ed sentinel sites w.e.f.  13-15 May 2010. Dte. of NVBDCP will continue to 
support the laboratories in terms of provision of JE kits free of cost from NIV, Pune. 
VII.  NATIONAL TB CONTROL PROGRAMME 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.1   Tuberculosis is a major public health problem  in India. The country accounts for nearly one-fift h of the global incidence. 
In 2007, out of the global annual incidence of 9.23  million TB cases, 1.96 million were estimated to h ave occurred in India, of 
whom 0.8 million were infectious cases. About two persons die from TB in India every three minutes; 900 people every day and 
almost 3.28 lakh every year. The Committee was info rmed that the Revised National TB Control Programme  (RNTCP) has 
been extended to the entire country in 2006. Till d ate, 11 million T.B. patients have been initiated o n treatment with 2 million 
additional lives saved. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.2   To tackle HIV/TB co-infection, TB + HIV colla borative activities with NACP, initiated in 2001, h ave been scaled up 
across the country. The Committee notes that intens ified TB/HIV package of services have been started in nine HIV high 
prevalence States (Andhra Pradesh, Goa, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Manipur, Mizoram, Nagaland, Pondicherry and Tamil Nadu) 
in 2008 and has been extended to nine additional States (Delhi, Gujarat, Punjab, Rajasthan, Kerala, Assam, West Bengal, Orissa 
and Chandigarh) in 2009-10 with a vision to cover t he entire nation by 2012. Monitoring of co-infected  patients was started 
from 4th quarter of 2008, and out of a total of 310 47 such cases, 5171 cases were treated with a 75 pe r cent success rate. The 
Committee is happy to note that the Department has been pursuing its concerns diligently to identify a nd provide treatment to 
HIV/TB co-infected patients. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  

7.3  To control this major health problem of the co untry, Revised National TB Control Programme (RNTCP ) based on 
internationally recommended strategy of Directly Observed Treatment Short Course (DOTS). The Programme has the objective 
of detecting at least 70% of cases and curing at le ast 85% of sputum positive patients. In order to ad dress the problem of Multi 
Drug Resistance TB (MDR- TB), establishment of a ne twork of 43 accredited Culture and Drug Susceptibil ity Testing 
Laboratories for diagnosis and follow-up of MDR-TB patients across the country was being undertaken in  a phased manner. It 
is planned to roll out services for Drug Resistant Tuberculosis in all State/ UTs by March 2011. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of record 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.4   The Committee observes that the emergence of multi-drug resistant TB has increased the severity and magnitude of the 
problem in terms of cost, longer and difficult trea tment regimen. In this context, the Committee welco mes the initiative of 
establishing a network of 43 Laboratories for diagn osis and follow-up of MDR-TB patients. The Committe e hopes that such 
Laboratories will be set up across the country in s uch a way that people from all parts of the country  have equity in access to 
such facilities. The Committee would like to have a n idea of the location of such Laboratories and als o of the structure of 
charges, if any, proposed to levied for availing of the facilities therefrom. 
Action Taken  
  The programme is establishing the proposed networ k of 43 laboratories at a site identified by the States with the objective 
of ensuring convenient and equitable access while m atching the expected workload in the particular Sta te. In addition, the 
programme is also accrediting and involving identif ied NGO/Private and Medical College laboratories to  supplement the 
diagnostic capacity and further facilitate ease of access. 
  In order to ensure that the patients do not have to travel long distances to access the laboratory services the programme is 
establishing efficient mechanisms for collection an d transportation of sputum samples from microscopy centres (close to the 
patient’s residence) to these laboratories. 
  The programme is providing the MDR-TB diagnostic and treatment services absolutely free of cost to all the patients. 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.5   The Committee observes that utilization statu s of allocated funds under the programme has been s atisfactory. For 2009-
10, against the total allocation of Rs.296.25 crore  an expenditure of Rs.294.83 crore has been made ti ll 12th March, 2010. The 
Committee is however, surprised to observe that uns pent balances are available with all the States/UTs  in the beginning of the 
year. The opening balance of Rs.32.20 crore as on 01.04.2009 has reached the figure of Rs.79.19 crore by 31st December, 2009.

crore at National level for 2009-10 calculated for each District is about Rs.5.30 lakh per District wh ich is the bare minimum

crore were released in the month of March (22.3.201 0). The balances with the States under reference ar e just sufficient enough 
to meet expenses till the time regular funds are ma de available. For further release of funds in the n ext financial year, these 
unspent balances are adjusted for release of first instalment.

Recommendations/Observations  

7.7   The Committee takes note of the fact that out  of a total outlay of Rs.1156 crore for the Programme, Rs.756.00 crore is the 
credit from World Bank. The Committee would like to  be enlightened if the credit from World Bank has a ny financial 
implications by way of debt or commitment charges and if so, the extent of financial liability involved so far. 
Action Taken  
  The World Bank credit of Rs.756 crores is a typog raphical error. It may be read as Rs.765 crores. 
(a) Rs.765 crore credit from the World Bank has bee n converted into loan currency of credit which is S pecial drawing 
Rights. The SDR value of the credit available to Go vt. of India is SDR 115.700 Million. Out of which t ill date 
disbursement under IDA Credit No. 4228-IN is SDR 56 .608 Million equivalent to Rs.389.55 crore. This in cludes 
Advance of US $15 million (Rs.67.00 crore). 
(b) Disbursement under this credit will be repaid i n 35 years with a grace period of 10 years in 50 se miannual 
instalments starting from 15.02.2017. 
(c)  Service Charges @ 0.75% p.a. will be levied by  the IDA on outstanding debt on half early basis. T ill date a sum of 
0.923 Million SDR equivalent to Rs.6.37 crore (0.68 4 Million SDR equivalent to RsA.85 crore on account  of 
Interest and 0.239 Million SDR equivalent to Rs.1.5 2 crore on account of Commitment Charges) has been paid as 
Service Charges interest and commitment charges. 
(d)  As per agreement, IDA is charging Commitment C harges @ 0.50% p.a. on undrawn balance which have a  waiver 
clause to be decided on year to year basis by the B oard of Director of the World Bank. However, at pre sent, IDA 
has waived entire Commitment Charges for the last t wo years and it will continue up to 30th June, 2010  or till 
further orders from World Bank. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.9   To the Committee’s query regarding the latest  status on the new drug for treating patient with MDR- TB being developed 
by M/s Johnson and Johnson, the Department has info rmed that the new molecule (TMC 207) is currently u ndergoing Phase II 
clinical trials, to evaluate its efficacy, safety a nd tolerability in patients suffering from MDR- TB.  Tuberculosis Research 
Centre, Chennai and All India Institute of Medical Sciences are participating in this trial which is b eing undertaken in 17 
countries across the world. The details on the cost, affordability, efficacy etc. will be available only after successful completion 
of the clinical trials and once the drug is approve d for marketing by the regulatory authorities. The Committee would like the 
Department to furnish the latest status on the Phase II clinical trials and its findings. 
Action Taken  
  As per the available information, the Phase II cl inical trails for the new molecule (TMC-207) are pr esently ongoing and 
the results are likely to be available by September 2010. 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.10   Committee’s attention has also been drawn by  Drug Resistance Surveys (DRS) undertaken in Gujara t and 
Maharashtra in 2005-06, which have estimated the pr evalence of Multi-drug Resistant TB (MDR- TB) to be  around 3% 
amongst new TB cases and 14-17% amongst re-treatment TB cases. It has also been informed that in Andhr a Pradesh the study 
has been completed and the results of which were ex pected to be available by Second quarter of 2010. I n U.P. and Orissa 
similar surveys were going on. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of Record 
Recommendations/Observations  
7.11   The Committee would like the Department to s hare with it the State-wise details of the Survey R eports while 
submitting its action taken note. The Committee has  been given to understand that a baseline social as sessment study at the 

beginning of the RNTCP II project had been undertaken to assess some of the predisposing factors contributing to the spread of 
the disease, and that another such survey is being planned to be made in 2010-11 which will also inclu de evaluation of the 
predisposing factors for spread of TB. The findings of the baseline survey may be shared with the Committee.  
Action Taken  
  DRS Survey Report:  State representative drug resistance surveillance (DRS) surveys have been undertaken in Gujarat 
and Maharashtra in 2005-06 and the results of these  surveys indicate that prevalence of MDR amongst ne w cases is 2.4%. 
(Published data from Gujarat) and 2.7% (Unpublished  data from Maharashtra). The prevalence of MDR amon gst re-treatment 
cases was found to be 14% (Unpublished data from Maharashtra) and 17.4% (Published data from Gujarat). 
  Three more DRS surveys are presently being undert aken in Andhra Pradesh, Western Uttar Pradesh and O rissa. AP has 
completed the study and the results are awaited. The Western UP study is underway and is likely to be completed by mid 2010. 
The pilot study for the DRS survey in Orissa has been successfully completed. The main study is yet to be started. 
  Social Assessment Study:  The objective of the study was to understand the s ocial context, cultural practices and social 
institutions within which the project is to be impl emented; understand the health seeking behavior of the marginalized and 
vulnerable sections and through this identify factors, which enhance benefits to the marginalized and vulnerable groups. 
  The study highlights that special attention has t o be paid to the local context and needs of the spe cial groups at the local 
level. Accessibility to services is an issue mostly in tribal areas. Need based, area specific interventions, with the involvement of 
local leader have been identified as the best optio n to cater the needs of the people. Use of local po pular media, local leaders, 
and tribal leaders has been identified in the study . Role and importance of CBOs, NGOs and PRIs has be en highlighted. Study 
also emphasizes need to strengthen public private partnership, more importantly in tribal areas.

conducted during NPCB during 2006-07 showed reducti on in the prevalence of blindness from 1.1 % (2001- 02) to 1 % (2006-
07).

Recommendations/Observations  
9.6   In reply to a specific query regarding the mo nitoring mechanism under NPCB for monitoring of Surgical Eye Camps, the 
Department has informed that such eye camps are not  permitted under NPCB. However, Screening Eye Camps  are conducted 
by NGOs/private Service Providers in the States wit h the approval of the concerned State Health author ities under their 
monitoring and strict supervision. The performance of these camps is reviewed at district, State and c entral level meetings. 
However, the above information is contrary to the findings reported in the latest CAG Performance Audit of NRHM (2009-10), 
as per which in 14 States/UTs, 19.52 lakh cataract surgeries were performed in camps during the period 2005-08 which was 47 
per cent of total cataract surgeries in these States. Position in the following States in quite alarming. 
Action Taken   
  Matter of Record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
9.12   The fact that as many as 10 States/UTs do no t have a single operational eye bank is difficult f or the Committee to 
accept. Another point that strikes a chord is the n umber of Eye Banks in the Government vis-a-vis  the private sector. The 
Committee notes that the ratio of eye banks operated in the private sector is 2.5 times the figure for the Government sector. The 
NPCB is into the fourth year since the launch of NRHM and between 2007-08 to 2009-10, nearly Rs.650 crores have been spent 
under the programme. The Committee finds that measures like non recurring assistance upto Rs.15 lakh to Eye Banks, recurring 
assistance of Rs.1500 per pair of eyes towards hono rarium of Eye Banks Staff, appointment of Eye donat ion Counsellors have 
also seemed to have made no impact.  
Action Taken  
  With the aim to promote Eye Banks in all States/U Ts in Government as well as in private sector, non-recurring assistance 
to Eye Banks has been enhanced from Rs.I0 lakh to R s.15 lakh during the Eleventh Plan Period. Recurrin g assistance of 
Rs.1500 per pair of eyes towards honorarium of Eye Banks Staff and salary for appointment of Eye donat ion Counselors are 
also provided to Eye Banks. In addition, Eye banks are being provided with MK Media for preservation of donated eyes, as per 
demand. New guidelines have been formulated under t he programme to promote eye banking activities thro ugh Government 
facilities. The promotional activities have been un dertaken with the aim to achieve encouraging result s by the end of Eleventh 
Plan. The Eye donation during the year 2009-10 has increased to 47,000 as compared to 41,090 during 2008-09.

of surveillance for communicable and non-communicab le diseases and to improve the efficiency of the ex isting surveillance

information furnished by the Ministry till 12th Mar ch, 2010 an expenditure of Rs.35.98 crore were incu rred. The Committee, 
however, notes that allocated funds during 2007-08 and 2008-09 have remained under-utilized.

been given to understand that 610 District surveill ance teams (cumulative since beginning of project i ncluding 202 teams of

were trained in two week long FETD programme and Di strict Surveillance Teams of all States/UTs covered  except Bihar,

Broad Band network was installed across 606 distric ts during 2008-09, while the only information furni shed on the progress 
during 2009-10 by the Ministry is that connectivity  on Broad Band network is being established through  National Informatics 
Network. Under the head of data collection and anal ysis, though some information regarding the data co llected and analysed 
from phase I, II and III states is available for 2008-09, no information is available in this regard during 2009-10.

Recommendations/Observations  
11.4   Regarding the query as to whether the requir ed trained manpower is available for the successful  implementation of 
this Programme, and if not the action plan in this regard, the Department has informed that training f or PHC doctors, Lab 
technicians, other paramedical staff, etc. has been completed in Phase I and II States and is being undertaken in Phase III States. 
The Department has also stated that a total of 766 Technical Consultants are to be recruited (1 Epidem iologist each at State 
Surveillance Unit (SSU) and District Surveillance U nit (DSU); 1 Microbiologist each at SSU and 50 prio rity District Labs; 1 
Entomologist each at all SSUs), of these, 261 Epide miologists, 29 Microbiologists and 10 Entomologists  have joined 
State/District Surveillance units for assisting the  State/ District health officers. Training of these  newly recruited health 
professionals has also been undertaken. The 2nd rou nd of recruitment of technical consultants for 466 remaining vacant posts 
will be done after getting the approval for restructuring and extension of IDSP. 
Action Taken  
  Matter of Record. 
Recommendations/Observations  
11.5  The Committee is happy to note that the recru itment and training of the required manpower under IDSP is proceeding at a 
positive pace. The Committee observes that the presence of trained surveillance manpower are key to the prevention and control 
of diseases/epidemic in such a large country as Ind ia. Hence, the Committee hopes that the targets of recruiting and training 
technical consultants for the remaining 400 vacant post would have been completed after due approval f or restructuring and 
extension of IDSP were obtained. The Committee, the refore, recommends the Department to furnish the de tails in this regard 
for its perusal. The Committee would also like to b e apprised of the progress made towards establishin g video conferencing at 
the remaining sites and strengthening the remaining priority labs. 
Action Taken  
  It is true that recruitment and training of the r equired manpower under IDSP is proceeding at a positive pace. 
  To expedite the recruitment, Ministry of Health a nd Family Welfare has decided that these recruitmen ts will be 
decentralized and the State Health Societies have b een authorized for filling up the contractual posit ions at the State/District 
level. 
  At present, a total of 359 out of the 400 sites h ave been installed by Indian Space Research Organiz ation (ISRO). The 
remaining 41 sites are yet to be installed. The mai n reasons for non-installation of these sites are n on-readiness of the sites, 
equipments short shipped, equipments lost after delivery. The States have been requested to expedite t he process of making the 
sites ready and restore the lost equipments after d elivery; for equipments short shipped, ISRO has been requested to deliver the 
equipments. 

  Strengthening of Public Health Laboratories:  50 priority district laboratories identified in 35  States/UT are being 
focused for strengthening in the country for labora tory diagnosis of epidemic prone diseases. A referr al laboratory network is 
being established in 9 priority States on a pilot b asis to provide access to diagnostic facilities during outbreaks in the remaining 
districts of the State. Comprehensive guidelines fo r the procurement of equipments for the priority di strict labs have been sent 
to all the States. The process of procurement in 16  States (22 labs) has been completed. Active follow -up action to monitor the 
process of procurement of equipments in the remaini ng identified priority labs is being done through t elephonic conversation, 
video conference and on site visits.

Recommendations/Observations  
2.13   The Committee is not prepared to accept the Department’s contention that delay in taking up exe cution of civil 
works due to State healthcare departments being dependent on PWD and other State agencies, was the mai n factor behind such 
a scenario. The other reasons like requirement of opening of Back Account at each level and lack of pr oper planning for under-
served areas are also equally unconvincing. The Com mittee strongly feels that these are all such matte rs which should have 
been resolved in the initial one or two years of NR HM. The Committee would also like to point out that  the monitoring and 
joint review missions of NRHM need to be followed b y resolving of major constraints like shortage of q ualified and trained 
manpower and lack of infrastructural facilities. Ot herwise allocated funds will continue to remain und er-utilized every year 
defeating the very purpose of launching of NRHM in a mission mode. 
Action Taken  
  The point regarding Civil Works is well taken. It  is being pursued with the States. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee is happy that the Department is pursu ing the matter of delay in civil works with the Sta tes. 
However, the Committee notes that the reply of the Department is silent about the measures taken to ti de over the 

shortage of trained and qualified manpower for prop er monitoring and the lack of infrastructural facil ities. The 
Committee would expect the Department to be more circumspect while furnishing the action taken note in future.  
III.  INFRASTUCTURE STRENGTHENING 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.4  The Committee is perturbed to find that out of  1,46,036 Sub-Centres, as many as 67233 Centre are functioning from 
rented buildings. From the details furnished by the  Department, the Committee finds that a large numbe r of States have simply 
failed to take benefit from Central funds being provided for construction upgradation of sub-centre buildings. In States/UTs like 
Himachal Pradesh, J&K, Tamil Nadu, A&N Islands, D&N  Haveli, Daman & Diu, Chandigarh, Lakshadweep, Pudu cherry no 
construction activity has been initiated so far. In  States which have taken some initiative in this re gard, percentage of sub-
centres being strengthened! upgraded is very low  
Action Taken  
  As per RHS 2009, the number of Sub-Centres functi oning from rented buildings has come down substantially from 50338 
to 47001. 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.5  The Committee is not convinced by the clarific ation given by the Department that in some States, sub-centres are working 
in other Government buildings/ spaces provided by P anchayats etc. The Committee also apprehends that c ondition of PHC and 
CHC buildings would also be no better. The fact rem ains that there is an urgent need for” undertaking a review of present 
condition of all the Sub-centre/ PHC/ CHC buildings  across the country and undertake the required cons truction under a time-
bound Action Plan. 
Action Taken  
  Point is noted. Efforts are being made under the NRHM approvals for States to focus on a building for every Sub-Centre. 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.6   The Committee’s attention is also drawn by th e damaging report given by the CAG in its latest re port on this crucial 
aspect. As per CAG report, among audited units, 216  sub-centres (16%) of 10 States and 19 PHCs (3% of 4 States, were 
operating without buildings 435 sub-centres (32%) of28 States/ UTs and 102 PHCs (15%) of 17 States and  7 CHCs of 4 States 
were operating in a rented building/Panchayat Bhawan/Others. 217 Sub-centres (16%) of 16 States/ UTs, 86 PHCs (13%) of 16 
States/ UTs and 23 CHCs (7%) of 5 States/ UTs were functioning in dilapidated buildings.  
Action Taken  
  Point regarding shortage of infrastructure is wel l taken. Ministry will make more efforts to improve  the infrastructure in 
States in partnership with the State Governments. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee feels that the Department is very c asual in its reply to a situation of serious concer n. The CAG 
Report findings about the status of Sub-centres, PHCs and CHCs could have been easily made the basis for initiating the 
required corrective measures. The Committee would appreciate if action is initiated in this direction by the Department 
in close co-ordination with State implementing authorities.  
3.17   Four Specialist doctors are required to be p osted at one CHC. The Committee finds that against the required number 
of 17104 Specialist for CHCs, sanctioned strength i s less than half i.e.  8376 and Specialists in-position are only 4279. St ate-
specific details are more disappointing. While no s pecialists have been posted in CHCs in Manipur, Miz oram, Delhi, 
Lakshadweep and Puducherry, no information is avail able in this regard with respect of Orissa, Tamil N adu and Tripura. The 
Committee is also constrained to observe that Speci alists recruited by the States fall short of their actual requirements.

  In all the above States, against the requirement of four Specialists per CHC, not even one Specialis t is available in all the 
CHCs.  
Action Taken  
  Matter of Record. 
 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee notes that as per the figures shown by the Department, there seems to be no significant increase in 
healthcare infrastructure and the numbers of person nel within a span of 5 years. As per the informatio n furnished 
above, between 2005 and 2009, the net increase in t he number of sub-centre is 352 only. The number of PHCs 
functioning from Government buildings has increased by 4213 only. The number of Pharmacists posted at PHC/ CHCs 
by 3295 and Radiographers at CHCs by 549 only. Comm ittee’s specific observation about acute shortage o f specialists 
required to be posted at CHCs has elicited no respo nse from the Department. The Committee observes tha t if the 
present requirements and the personnel posted are matched up to the in-position figures, the deficienc ies would be even 
more glaring. The Committee takes due cognition of the fact that creating a brigade of healthcare personnel is no doubt 
a time taking process, but it observes that 5 years ’ time is sufficient to bring about a visible chang e in their numbers. 
The Committee suggests that Mission Directors should be impressed upon to take up with the States on u rgent basis the 
issue of recruitment and posting of personnel as well as bridging the shortfall in infrastructure to meet the targets set up 
under NRHM within a reasonable time frame. The Comm ittee would like to be kept apprised of the specifi c steps taken 
by the Department to overcome the shortage of health functionaries, and for strengthening the infrastructure. 
Recommendations/Observations  
3.18  Status of ANMs at Sub-Centres and PHCs  
  The Committee is also quite disappointed with the  progress made over the past four years regarding f illing up the vacant 
posts of the various healthcare functionaries under  NRHM. The Committee observes that one of the most important objective 
with which the Mission was launched at the outset w as to ensure the availability of the various health care functionaries, i.e.  
ANMs/ MPWs(M) /LHVs/ Lab Technicians, doctors and s pecialists at Sub-Centres, PHCs and CHCs at the rig ht places. 
Realizing that their presence is essential for the smooth functioning of health centres and hence, important for the success of the 
Mission, the Committee has been recommending to the  Department year after year to take up the issue of  non-filling up of the 
vacancies with the States/UTs. However, there seems  to be no significant improvement in the situation.  The Committee feels 
that four years of focused attention, and considerable infusion of funds under NRHM, aimed to create a brigade of healthcare is 

a long enough time for the situation to have improv ed. Neither the flow of funds nor the efforts of th e Department are found 
wanting. The only conclusion the Committee can draw is that required manpower for the Health Centres is simply not available 
in majority of the States. 
Action Taken  
  As the RHS comparison of 2005-09 has indicated th ere has been a substantial increase in health human  resources in the 
primary healthcare system. While acknowledging the gap which still exists, the Ministry would like to place on record that over 
7 lakh community workers and more than 1 lakh health workers have been added to the health system under NRHM. Filling up 
of full human resource gaps require longer time span as simultaneous efforts to increase seats in medi cal and nursing education 
have also to be made. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  It is a fact that considerable manpower has been created under NRHM so far. However, the Committee i s not 
convinced by the contention of the Department that a longer time span is required for filling up of human resource gaps. 
One must not forget that NRHM is mandated to be implemented in a mission mode.

4.6   The Committee observes that while Tamil Nadu has been able to operationalise all its PHCs 24x7, no other State has been 
able to achieve such a feat. The Committee observes  that the status of PHCs running 24x7 in some of th e States need to be 
revisited. States indicated above need to double up  their efforts so as to achieve the targets of maki ng all the PHCs operational 
24x7. The Committee fails to understand as to what could be the hindrance, especially when funds are available for the purpose. 
The Committee recommends the Department to take up the matter of increasing the number of PHCs operati ng 24x7 as well as 
up gradation of PHCs/ CHCs to FRUs with all the Sta tes/UTs so that a critical service like Health Care  is available to the 
targeted beneficiaries wherever required. 
Action Taken  
  The suggestion of the Hon’ble Members of the Comm ittee is very appropriate. Given the constraints of  human resources 
and physical infrastructure, it has not been possib le to upgrade all PHCs into 24x7 at one go. More ef forts will be made in this 
direction in the commg years. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  While the Committee appreciates the fact that the  Department intends to make more efforts to upgrade  greater 
number of PHCs into 24x7, it would like the Departm ent to move in this direction in a structured way. The Committee 
would, therefore, like the Department to formulate a concrete and time-bound action plan for upgrading  all PHCs into 
24x7. The Committee does understand the major const raints in achieving the said objective of human res ource and 
physical infrastructure. Accordingly, the Committee impresses upon the Department to address these two basic issues on 
war footing.

be tried in the coming years.

intervention under NRHM, designed to provide assure d quality health services to the people. Upgradatio n work of

Committee, therefore, urges upon the Department to indicate the specific efforts made by it to speed u p the pace of 
upgradation of physical infrastructure. The Committ ee observes that the Department should abandon the lackadaisical 
attitude in the matter and come up with a specific plan to get the CHCs accredited at the earliest and  urges the 
Department to come up with a more elaborate reply in the matter.

4.18   Training of ASHAs  
  The Committee views with serious concern the gap in meeting the targets of ASHA training upto IVth Module in most of 
the States. The Committee is not convinced by the reason furnished by the Department that the delay is only confined to the 4th 
Module training in ASHAs in some of the States. Kee ping in view the key role ASHA plays in achievement  of various NRHM 
objectives, these gaps need to be addressed with a sense of urgency. The Committee observes that given  the low level of 
availability of health care services in the rural a reas of high focused States, they cannot afford to be off-target in such a crucial 
area as training to ASHA. The Committee, therefore,  recommends to the Department to impress upon all t he States/UTs, 
particularly those lagging behind, to take immediat e corrective steps to meet the targets fixed in thi s regard. The Committee 
would like to be apprised of the state-wise updated  status of progress made towards bridging the gaps in the IVth Module 
training of ASHA.  
Action Taken  
  All efforts to further speed up the training of A SHAs is being made. The progress up to the fourth m odule is quite 
satisfactory in most States except Bihar. The roll out of the fifth module has taken some time as it w as mandated that it will be 
carried out only through Non-Governmental Organizat ions. The State-wise updated status of training of ASHAs is at 
Annexure- VI . 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee takes notes of the submission of the Department that the roll-out of the fifth module ha s taken 
some time as it was mandated to be carried out only  through Non-Governmental Organisations. The Commit tee feels 
that delay in the roll out of the fifth module is indicative of the shortcomings in the coordination mechanism between the 
Department and the State Governments. The Committee  also feels that these issues should have been addr essed in the 
project conceptualization stage itself. The trainin g of ASHAs is an imminent necessity for accomplishm ent of the goals 
set under NRHM. The Committee feels distressed that  despite its repeated recommendations, the Departme nt has not 
been able to achieve the desired level of capacity building of ASHAs. The Committee once again urges u pon the 
Department to immediately initiate appropriate corr ective measures to complete the fifth module of tra ining of ASHAs 
in all the States.

with regard to the disparity in the completion of t raining modules of ASHAs and distribution of drug k its to them. The 
Committee observes that there seems to be no set pr actice being followed regarding distribution of dru g kits and the 
level of training of ASHAs. The Committee would lik e the Department to clarify the reasons for such un even 
implementation of this initiative across the country. The Department should make all out efforts to pr ovide drug kits to 
all trained ASHAs.

work of reviewing the ‘job responsibilities’ and ‘R ole clarity of ASHA’ as well as that of A WW and AN M. On being asked 
about the latest status on the matter and to furnis h details thereof, the Department has informed that  NHSRC has reviewed the 
job responsibilities and role clarity of ASHA. To ensure that the current roles played by ASHA in various States are reflected in 
redefining the role clarity and job responsibilitie s, a process of consultations and reviews was under taken. These include 
Consultations with ASHA Monitoring Group (AMG) members, reviewing the reports of the Common Review Mission, decision 
of various consultative meetings, and reports of Ph ase I evaluation of ASHA programme done in eight St ates namely Assam, 
Andhra Pradesh, Kerala, Jharkhand, Rajasthan, Bihar , Orissa and West Bengal. Based on these consultati ons and reviews, a 
note on the role of ASHA, her responsibilities and payment mechanisms has been prepared by NHSRC. The Department has 
further stated that NHSRC is in final stage of preparing its Report on review of job responsibilities and role clarity of ANM and 
AWW which would be available by March 2010. 
Action Taken  
  The report has since been submitted and is presen tly under examination.

4.29   Mobile Medical Units  
  The Committee is not very convinced by the Depart ment’s replies regarding procurement and provision of MMU s. The 
Department has informed that States, based on their need and the resources put forth their demands for MMUs in the state PIPs. 
However, the Committee feels that the Department sh ould take active interest in promoting and providin g MMUs to the 
uncovered/under-covered districts of the country. T he Committee notes that States/UTs like U.P, Chhatt isgarh and Himachal 
Pradesh are some of the States where the health car e indices are already on slippery ground. However, with not even a single 
MMU available in any of the district in the aforesa id States one can imagine the status of accessibili ty of healthcare services in 
under-served/unserved areas. Non-operationalisation / inadequate functioning of MMUs aggravated the pro blem further. The 
Committee observes that the Department would have t o devise other means and methods beyond mere pursua nce with the 
States, to impress the need for the presence of MMUs at the district level so that some emergency medical services can be made 
available to needy. 
Action Taken  
  It was important in Mobile Medical Units for the States to take full ownership of it. That is why fl exibility was provided 
to the States to choose the Mobile Medical Unit ide al model. It is also an issue worth considering whe ther all districts actually 
need a Mobile Medical Unit. Perhaps a better case w ould be to intensively provide MMUs in inaccessible  districts. Such 
districts will actually require many more MMUs and they need not be spread to all the districts of the country. 
Recommendations/Observations  

4.30   Of the 354 MMUs operational in the country, the fact as to how many of them are actually functi onal is unclear. 
Moreover, it is also unclear as to whether any asse ssment has been made to find out that the MMUs purc hased till date confirm 
to the standards. CAG findings in its latest report  are quite alarming. The Committee notes that MMUs procured in Manipur 
remained unutilized as of October, 2008 for want of  technical know-how and Specialist doctors. Similar ly, MMUs were duly 
procured by Tripura but remained unutilized. Expres sion of interest was floated in May, 2008 inviting interested NGOs/ 
Charitable Organisations for operationalising the MMUs.

  Noted for compliance

scenario, the Committee strongly feels that the Min istry needs to undertake a country-wide review abou t functional status of 
MMUs. Thereafter, constant monitoring at regular in tervals has to be carried out so as to ensure the a ccessibility of healthcare 
facilities to the general masses. 
Action Taken  
  Noted for compliance. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee notes that this is a classic case of the fact that wisdom dawns on hindsight. The Commit tee 
observes that after so much of funds have been infu sed into a healthcare initiative, the assets so cre ated by allocating 
scarce resources seems to be lying idle and unused in so many of the States across the country. The Co mmittee observes 
that if the Department had acted with prudence and foresight, then such a waste of money could have been avoided. The 
Department has not informed the Committee the actua l number of MMUs functional across the country and as to 
whether how many NGOs/Charitable Hospitals have exp ressed interest to operationalise the MMUs. The Com mittee

V.  RCH FLEXIBLE POOL 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.16   From a perusal of the information made avail able, the Committee would like to state here that s ignificant progress 
under the Immunization Programme has not been noticed in many of the States. This fact holds true both for the high focus and 
EAG States and also the non-high focus States. In s ome instances like that of Tamil Nadu and Dadra & N agar Haveli, the 
immunization coverage has infact dropped below the DLHS II rate. The Committee observes that a low cov erage rate in some 
of the very densely populated States like Bihar and Uttar Pradesh means that a very large number of children remain exposed to 
the vagaries of diseases resulting in high incidence of IMR in the country. The Committee observes that unless proper execution 
of the plans are made by the programme authorities,  visible impact on the immunization coverage would remain a distant 
dream. It, therefore, urges the Department to take up the issue with the respective State Mission Dire ctors during the next 
Common Review Mission, who need to be impressed upon to achieve the required quarterly/ annual targets. 
Action Taken  
  As per the DLHS-2 data, the last 5 worst performi ng States have been the State of Bihar, Meghalaya, Arunachal Pradesh, 
Nagaland and Manipur. As per DLHS-3, all these Stat es of high focus and EAG has shown significant impr ovement in the 
coverage ( Annexure-IX & X ). However, there is further scope for improvement of the coverage in these States. In order to 
further strengthen the activity, all the State Miss ion Directors were called for the meeting on 12-13 April 2010 and the matter 
was discussed. The Common Review Mission has been reviewing the same and will take up the matter in the next review. In the 

good performing States like Tamil Nadu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli, the performance has shown some declin e and they will 
also be the focus to improve coverage. 
COMMENTS  OF  THE  COMMITTEE  
  The Committee is happy to note that in some of the crucial states like J&K (DLHS-2-32.0- DLHS-3-63.0),  
Uttarakhand (DLHS2-44.5 - DLHS3-63.3), West Bengal (DLHS2-50.3- DLHS3-75.8), Sikkim (DLHS2-52.7 - DLHS -3-
77.9), Daman & Diu (DLHS2-56.1 - DLHS-3-83.5), Raja sthan (DLHS-2-23.0-DLHS-3-50.00), Assam (DLHS-2-16. 0-
DLHS-3-51.00), Bihar (DLHS-2-20.7-DLHS-3-41.4) ther e has been significant improvement in immunization coverage. 
However, the Committee is not aware whether there i s any mechanism for surprise inspections so as to a ssess the exact 
position at the ground level. The Committee would a ppreciate if an initiative is taken for cross-check ing the data 
furnished by these States. Committee’s attention ha s also been drawn by the downward trend noticed in the 
immunization coverage in States like Tamil Nadu (DLHS2-91.4 - DLHS-3-81.6) and Maharashtra (DLHS2-70.9 - DLHS-
3-69.3). The Committee also notes with serious conc ern only marginal increase in the immunization cove rage in States 
like UP(DLHS2-25.8 -DLHS-3-31.1), Madhya Pradesh (D LHS2-30.4 - DLHS-3-38.5), Tripura (DLHS2-32.6 - DLH S-3-
38.5), Gujarat (DLHS2-54.0 - DLHS-3-55.5), Haryana (DLHS2-59.1 - DLHS-3-61.8) and Kerala (DLHS2-78.5- DLHS-3-
79.0). The Committee is of the view that Immunizati on Programme needs to be vigorously pursued in Stat es like UP, 
Madhya Pradesh, Tripura, Meghalaya, Arunachal Prade sh, Bihar, Manipur, Rajasthan where coverage remain s below 
the national average. 
Recommendations/Observations  
5.17   Cold chain maintenance is the most crucial c omponent for effective implementation of the Immuni sation 
Programme. The Committee, however, observes that so  far this has proved to be the weakest link under t he programme. 
Committee’s apprehensions have been confirmed by th e assessment of cold chain maintenance arrangement made under the 
Performance Audit of CAG. As per the CAG Audit, non e of the cold chain equipment was available in any of the 124 test 
checked PHCs in Haryana, Meghalaya, Madhya Pradesh,  Puducherry and West Bengal. Besides, in Bihar, Lak shadweep and 
Uttar Pradesh, 31 to 68 per cent of cold chain equipment was found non-functional. 
Action Taken  
  The vaccines are stored in the temperature betwee n 2-8°C in all levels upto PHCs. The cold chain equ ipment i.e.  fridge 
and/or Ice Line Refrigerator storage are used to ma intain this temperature for the vaccine. The advant age of Ice Line 
Refrigerator is that even in case of electricity fa ilure, it can hold the temperature for 72 hours. No rmally an Ice Line 
Refrigerator would require 8 hrs of power supply pe r day to maintain the requisite temperature. The fr idge does not have such 
long hold over time. The States have been using fri dge or Ice Line Refrigerator for storing vaccine. T he deep freezers are used 
for freezing ice packs which are then used in the vaccine carrier for transportation of the vaccine on a session to session basis to 
outreach session site or to other site where the vaccines are not stored. 
  As per the performance audit of CAG (Report No. 8  of 2009-10) conducted in 2007-08 Para 6.5.5 (Annexure-Xl ) none of 
the cold chain equipment was available in any of th e 124 test checked PHCs in Haryana, Meghalaya, Madh ya Pradesh, 
Puducherry and West Bengal. It seems that these PHCs are functioning as outreach session without having storage facility. The 
audit report has not enclosed the list of these 124 PHCs. 
  Instructions have been issued from time to time t o the State governments to get all the serviceable cold chain equipments 
repaired and ensure that no equipment is kept for more than one month for want of repair. 
COMMENTS  OF  THE  COMMITTEE  
  The Committee is surprised by the response of the D epartment to the concern shown by it on non-availab ility of 
cold chain equipment as pointed out in the CAG Repo rt. The Committee doubts whether any follow-up acti on has been 
initiated by the Department on the findings of CAG Report. The Committee would appreciate if the list of 124 PHCs 
inspected by CAG is obtained from it without any further delay and corrective measures are taken accordingly. 
Recommendations/Observations  

5.18   The Committee is more disturbed by the fact that Government of India budgetary support for main tenance of cold 
chain equipments, supply of spare parts, provision of alternate power supply has proved to be of no av ail. The States have 
simply confirmed their inability to absorb the fund s for envisaged purposes. The Committee is of the v iew that the absence of 
cold chain management has indirectly resulted in adverse impact on the UIP, a high priority area under the Mission.  
Action Taken  
  The Government of India has been providing the bu dgetary support for maintenance of cold chain, supply of spares which 
are being used by the States for repair and mainten ance of the cold chain equipment lying with the Sta te governments. The 
provision for alternate power supply has been made to cover-up the period of electricity failure and e nsuring cold chain 
maintenance. The funds utilized by the various Stat es under this provision are annexed in Annexure-XII . It can be seen that 
most of the States have been utilizing the said funds for maintenance of the cold chain. 
COMMENTS OF THE COMMITTEE  
  The Committee notes, from the information given in Annexure XII, that the utilization of funds by as m any as 
eleven States/ UTs namely, D&N Haveli, Daman & Diu,  Delhi, Jharkhand, Maharashtra, Meghalaya, Puducher ry, 
Punjab, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh and Ut tarakhand for repair and maintenance of the cold ch ain 
equipments ranges between 1.5% to 37.9% which by any standard cannot be termed satisfactory. The Committee views 
this trend with serious concern as the under-utilis ation of funds could have serious implication on th e proper storage of 
vaccines and the resultant efficacy thereof. The Co mmittee would, therefore, like the Department to ta ke appropriate 
remedial measures in this regard for making the UIP successful. 
Recommendations/Observations

Performance Audit of NRHM (2009-10) has pointed out a number of shortcomings in the implementation of JSY, out of which 
some are as follows:--- 
(i)  In 13 out of 20 States, less than 50 per cent of total registered pregnant women preferred instit utional delivery at 
health centres. Details of following States are self-revealing.

  delivery
