nothing but outrightly rejecting a good initiative on technical grounds. Had the Department been serio us on this 
suggestion, it would have seized the initiative and started a similar initiative at the Central level whereby all other States 
could be funded from the central pool for undertaking similar initiatives for the poor people.  (Para 21.8)  
(NRHM) 
II.  BUDGETARY PROVISIONS 

  The Committee notes that the cumulative expenditu re figures for the above three programmes, as given  in 
Annexure-I of the ATN, show a promising trend of increase. Yet it is also a fact that the expenditure figures for Routine 
Immunization Programme for the year 2008-09 are too  dismal to be ignored. Large sums of money have bee n left 
unspent under Routine Immunization Programme during 2008-09. The Committee fails to understand as to why a mere 
27.08% of funds could be utilized under Routine Immunization Programme during 2008-09. 
  The Committee is disappointed to note that instea d of apprising it of a specific action plan to chec k the under-
utilisation of funds, the Department has made a gen eric statement that all out efforts are being made to increase the 
absorptive capacity of States where the utilization  is less than the allocation. The. Committee, there fore, suggests that 
the Department should assess the reasons for its failure in monitoring the funds utilisation under these programmes and 
devise means to remove the loopholes, to ensure optimum utilization of funds.  (Para 2.1)  
  The Committee is happy that the Department is pur suing the matter of delay in civil works with the S tates. 
However, the Committee notes that the reply of the Department is silent about the measures taken to ti de over the 
shortage of trained and qualified manpower for prop er monitoring and the lack of infrastructural facil ities. The 
Committee would expect the Department to be more circumspect while furnishing the action taken note in future. 
        (Para 2.13)  
III.  INFRASTUCTURE STRENGTHENING 
  The Committee feels that the Department is very c asual in its reply to a situation of serious concer n. The CAG 
Report findings about the status of Sub-centres, PHCs and CHCs could have been easily made the basis for initiating the 
required corrective measures. The Committee would appreciate if action is initiated in this direction by the Department 
in close co-ordination with State implementing authorities.  (Para 3.4, 3.5 & 3.6)  
  The Committee notes that as per the figures shown  by the Department, there seems to be no significant increase in 
health care infrastructure and the numbers of perso nnel within a span of 5 years. As per the informati on furnished 
above, between 2005 and 2009, the net increase in t he number of sub-centre is 352 only. The number of PHCs 
functioning from Government buildings has increased by 4213 only. The number of Pharmacists posted at PHC/ CHCs 
by 3295 and Radiographers at CHCs by 549 only. Comm ittee’s specific observation about acute shortage o f specialists 
required to be posted at CHCs has elicited no respo nse from the Department. The Committee observes tha t if the 
present requirements and the personnel posted are matched up to the in-position figures, the deficienc ies would be even 
more glaring. The Committee takes due cognition of the fact that creating a brigade of health care personnel is no doubt 
a time taking process, but it observes that 5 year' s time is sufficient to bring about a visible chang e in their numbers. 
The Committee suggests that Mission Directors should be impressed upon to take up with the States on u rgent basis the 
issue of recruitment and posting of personnel as well as bridging the shortfall in infrastructure to meet the targets set up 
under NRHM within a reasonable time frame. The Comm ittee would like to be kept apprised of the specifi c steps taken 
by the Department to overcome the shortage of health functionaries, and for strengthening the infrastructure.  
           (Para 3.17)  
  It is a fact that considerable manpower has been created under NRHM so far. However, the Committee i s not 
convinced by the contention of the Department that a longer time span is required for filling up of human resource gaps. 
One must not forget that NRHM is mandated to be implemented in a mission mode.  (Para 3.18)  
IV.  MISSION  FLEXIBLE  POOL 
  While the Committee appreciates the fact that the  Department intends to make more efforts to upgrade  greater 
number of PHCs into 24x7, it would like the Departm ent to move in this direction in a structured way. The Committee 
would, therefore, like the Department to formulate a concrete and time-bound action plan for upgrading  all PHCs into 
24x7. The Committee does understand the major const raints in achieving the said objective of human res ource and 
physical infrastructure. Accordingly, the Committee impresses upon the Department to address these two basic issues on 
war footing.     (Para 4.6)  

  The Committee observes that upgradation of CHCs t o Indian Public Health Standards is an important 
intervention under NRHM, designed to provide assure d quality health services to the people. Upgradatio n work of 
CHCs in States/ UTs like Bihar, Chhattisgarh, Jhark hand, Himachal Pradesh, Andhra Pradesh, D&N Haveli,  
Chandigarh, Lakshadweep and Delhi were yet to be st arted at the beginning of the year. However, the De partment has 
not furnished any information as to why such an imp ortant initiative has been set on the backfoot. The  Committee feels 
that the reply given by the Department is generic i n nature and gives no idea of the specific and conc rete action being 
taken or contemplated to be taken to meet the targe t of upgradation of CHCs to IPHS. With only two yea rs of the 
Mission period being left, the action plan regardin g accreditation of the CHCs should have been starte d earlier. The 
Committee, therefore, urges upon the Department to indicate the specific efforts made by it to speed u p the pace of 
upgradation of physical infrastructure. The Committ ee observes that the Department should abandon the lackadaisical 
attitude in the matter and come up with a specific plan to get the CHCs accredited at the earliest and  urges the 
Department to come up with a more elaborate reply in the matter.  (Para 4.7 & 4.8)  
  While making the above observation, the Committee  had expected the Department to initiate some corre ctive 
measures so that greater transparency is ensured in  the functioning of Rogi Kalyan Samitis. The Commit tee, therefore, 
urges the Department to take urgent corrective meas ures in this regard and keep the Committee apprised  of the results 
achieved.        (Para 4.11 & 4.12)  
  The Committee takes notes of the submission of th e Department that the roll-out of the fifth module has taken 
some time as it was mandated to be carried out only  through Non-Governmental Organisations. The Commit tee feels 
that delay in the roll out of the fifth module is indicative of the shortcomings in the coordination mechanism between the 
Department and the State Governments. The Committee  also feels that these issues should have been addr essed in the 
project conceptualization stage itself. The trainin g of ASHAs is an imminent necessity for accomplishm ent of the goals 
set under NRHM. The Committee feels distressed that  despite its repeated recommendations, the Departme nt has not 
been able to achieve the desired level of capacity building of ASHAs. The Committee once again urges u pon the 
Department to immediately initiate appropriate corr ective measures to complete the fifth module of tra ining of ASHAs 
in all the States.     (Para 4.18)  
  From a perusal of the data furnished in the Annex ure VI and VII, the Committee notes that by May, 20 10, the 
second-module training of ASHAs could not be comple ted in Tamil Nadu and Chandigarh, the third module could not 
be completed in Himachal Pradesh. In case of Punjab , Kerala, Haryana, West Bengal and Maharashtra the story is no 
different. The Committee fails to understand the re asons behind such. a poor display of achievements b y the above 
States/ UTs. The Committee finds that there is larg e disparity in the distribution of drug kit among t he States 
irrespective of the number of training module compl eted by them. In case of Bihar, while 49 per cent o f ASHAs 
completed training in fourth module no drug kit has  been issued to any of them. Whereas in Himachal Pr adesh while 
only 36 ASHAs out of a total of 2393 selected could complete first module drug kits had been provided to all the selected 
ASHAs. The case of West Bengal, Gujarat, Haryana, P unjab, Tamil Nadu and Maharashtra seems to be no di fferent 
with regard to the disparity in the completion of t raining modules of ASHAs and distribution of drug k its to them. The 
Committee observes that there seems to be no set pr actice being followed regarding distribution of dru g kits and the 
level of training of ASHAs. The Committee would lik e the Department to clarify the reasons for such un even 
implementation of this initiative across the country. The Department should make all out efforts to pr ovide drug kits to 
all trained ASHAs.    (Para 4.21)  
  The Committee is not happy with the vague and eva sive reply given by the Department to its specific observations/ 
recommendations pertaining to the most crucial functionary of NRHM. The Committee would like to be apprised of the 
action initiated by the Department on its specific suggestion to provide ASHA as remuneration a minimu m amount per 
month as allowance as was recommended by the Missio n Steering Group. The Committee would like to be ap prised of 
the findings of the National Health System Resource Centre (NHSRC) Report at the earliest.         (Para 4.24 & 4.25)  
  The Committee notes that this is a classic case o f the fact that wisdom dawns on hindsight. The Comm ittee 
observes that after so much of funds have been infu sed into a health care initiative, the assets so cr eated by allocating 
scarce resources seems to be lying idle and unused in so many of the States across the country. The Co mmittee observes 

that if the Department had acted with prudence and foresight, then such a waste of money could have been avoided. The 
Department has not informed the Committee the actua l number of MMUs functional across the country and as to 
whether how many NGOs/Charitable Hospitals have exp ressed interest to operationalise the MMUs. The Com mittee 
would like the Department to inform it about the latest status in this regard while examining Demands for Grants of the 
forthcoming year. The Committee would also like to be enlightened as to whether any proposal has been firmed up for 
providing more MMUs in the inaccessible districts of the country.      (Para 4.29, 4.30 & 4.31)  
V.  RCH FLEXIBLE POOL 
  The Committee is happy to note that in some of th e crucial states like J&K (DLHS-2-32.0- DLHS-3-63.0 ), 
Uttarakhand (DLHS2-44.5 - DLHS3-63.3), West Bengal (DLHS2-50.3- DLHS3-75.8), Sikkim (DLHS2-52.7 - DLHS -3-
77.9), Daman & Diu (DLHS2-56.1 - DLHS-3-83.5), Raja sthan (DLHS-2-23.0-DLHS-3-50.00), Assam (DLHS-2-16. 0-
DLHS-3-51.00), Bihar (DLHS-2-20.7-DLHS-3-41.4) ther e has been significant improvement in immunization coverage. 
However, the Committee is not aware whether there i s any mechanism for surprise inspections so as to a ssess the exact 
position at the ground level. The Committee would a ppreciate if an initiative is taken for cross-check ing the data 
furnished by these States. Committee’s attention ha s also been drawn by the downward trend noticed in the 
immunization coverage in States like Tamil Nadu (DLHS2-91.4 - DLHS-3-81.6) and Maharashtra (DLHS2-70.9 - DLHS-
3-69.3). The Committee also notes with serious conc ern only marginal increase in the immunization cove rage in States 
like UP(DLHS2-25.8 -DLHS-3-31.1), Madhya Pradesh (D LHS2-30.4 - DLHS-3-38.5), Tripura (DLHS2-32.6 - DLH S-3-
38.5), Gujarat (DLHS2-54.0 - DLHS-3-55.5), Haryana (DLHS2-59.1 - DLHS-3-61.8) and Kerala (DLHS2-78.5- DLHS-3-
79.0). The Committee is of the view that Immunizati on Programme needs to be vigorously pursued in Stat es like UP, 
Madhya Pradesh, Tripura, Meghalaya, Arunachal Prade sh, Bihar, Manipur, Rajasthan where coverage remain s below 
the national average.    (Para 5.16)  
  The Committee is surprised by the response of the  Department to the concern shown by it on non-avail ability of 
cold chain equipment as pointed out in the CAG Repo rt. The Committee doubts whether any follow-up acti on has been 
initiated by the Department on the findings of CAG Report. The Committee would appreciate if the list of 124 PHCs 
inspected by CAG is obtained from it without any fu rther delay and corrective measures are taken accor dingly. 
        (Para 5.17)  
  The Committee notes, from the information given i n Annexure XII, that the utilization of funds by as  many as 
eleven States/UTs namely, D&N Haveli, Daman & Diu, Delhi, Jharkhand, Maharashtra, Meghalaya, Puducherr y, 
Punjab, Rajasthan, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh and Ut tarakhand for repair and maintenance of the cold ch ain 
equipments ranges between 1.5% to 37.9% which by any standard cannot be termed satisfactory. The Committee views 
this trend with serious concern as the under-utilis ation of funds could have serious implication on th e proper storage of 
vaccines and the resultant efficacy thereof. The Co mmittee would, therefore, like the Department to ta ke appropriate 
remedial measures in this regard for making the UIP successful.  (Para 5.18)  
  The Committee takes due cognition of the efforts cited by the Department for promoting institutional  deliveries. 
However, what draws the Committee’s attention is th e fact that the Department has been silent about th e shortcomings 
pointed out by the CAG in its Performance Audit of the NRHM (2009-10). Inspite of the above interventi ons and 
monitoring mechanisms if the loopholes pointed out by CAG persist then the objective of such an important intervention 
like Janani Suraksha Yojana to reduce MMR in the co untry would stand diluted. Involvement of Voluntary  
Organisations/NGOs working in the field of maternal  health should be sought to track the number of ben eficiaries 
opting for institutional delivery and the number of  beneficiaries under this programme. Even audit of records of 
payments to the beneficiaries should be handed over  to the independent agency to bring forth greater f inancial 
accountability into the programme. In order to incr ease institutional deliveries the Department should  first identify the 
district in a State with very high MMR and thereaft er, should carry out a campaign to track down pregn ant women in 
the area and counsel and persuade them to avail the benefits of the programme. This would be a beginning which can be 
replicated in other areas/districts/states by givin g credibility to the service guarantees envisaged t o be provided to the 
needy pregnant women under the programme and the resultant outcome, i.e., reduction in the MMR.  
                 (Para 5.26 & 5.27)  

  The Committee observes that the Evaluation Study of JSY being conducted by the National Health Syste ms 
Resource Centre is yet to start as only required fo rmalities have been completed so far. The Committee  would like to 
emphasize that this Evaluation Study needs to be co mpleted at the earliest so that problem areas can b e identified and 
corrective action taken accordingly.  (Para 5.28)  
VI.   NATIONAL VECTOR BORNE DISEASE CONTROL PROGRAM ME (NVBDCP) 
  The Committee does understand that the allocation  is made on Annual Plan basis but the fact remains that the 
Department has chosen to remain silent on the issue of underutilisation of funds. The Committee would like to point out 
that total Plan outlay in respect of any scheme is based on the specific requirements, both existing and anticipated. Ideal 
position is to go for optimum and judicious utilisation of allocated funds with the objective of earmarked funds being put 
to use as per the envisaged physical targets. The C ommittee, accordingly, urges upon the Department to  take urgent 
remedial steps to address the issue to ward off any negative impact on the programme.  (Para 6.3)  
  The Committee notes that though the Department ha s identified three clear reasons for reduced utiliz ation of the 
allocated fund under this head, its reply lacks cla rity inasmuch as it fails to indicate substantive m easures taken to 
overcome the problem. The Committee would, however,  like to be apprised about the outcome of the remed ial action 
taken by it to address this issue.  (Para 6.4)  
  The Committee observes that there is considerable  gap with regard to District Malaria Officers in tw o N-E States, 
i.e. Arunachal Pradesh and Assam. Out of 16 districts of Arunachal Pradesh, District Malaria Officer is posted in only 3 
districts. Similarly, only 13 districts out of tota l 27 districts of Assam have DMOs. The Committee wo uld appreciate if 
concerted efforts are made by the Department in pursuing this crucial issue with both these two states. 
  Another pressing issue that the Committee would l ike to place on record is that since most of the tr ibal areas are 
highly endemic with Malaria, the outreach of effect ive door to door surveillance services needs to be strengthened and 
carried out diligently. In order to ensure this, th e Committee recommends that the Department devise a  mechanism to 
carry out a monthly review of the surveillance carried out at the district level and to visit specific tribal pockets in order 
to gain first-hand information of the ground realities.  (Para 6.9)  
KALA-AZAR 
  Kala-azar had remained a serious problem for the country for quite some time. The Department has rep lied in its 
ATN that no NGO/ FBO/ PRI is involved in Kala-azar elimination activities. The Committee feels that th e proper 
incentives, support and counseling to the above ent ities would guarantee their involvement in the elim ination of the 
Kala-azar. The Committee fully agrees that for unde rtaking extensive search from house to house, a lar ge number of 
persons are required. Hence, it suggests that the P RIs could be used to be an effective mechanism of a ugmenting 
manpower for door to door searching and case detection. Hence, the Department should devise means of involving PRIs 
in Kala-azar elimination activities. The Committee suggests that the Department should also explore po ssibility of 
involving! contracting out such activities to the p rivate entities. The Committee hopes that the Kala- azar rounds must 
have been carried out during 2010. The Committee would like to be apprised about the latest status in this regard. 
       (Para 6.13 & 6.14)  
JAP ANESE ENCEPHALITIS 
  The Committee is not convinced by the Department’ s reply that apart from Adilabad district in Andhra  Pradesh 
and two districts of Goa and West Bengal each, the vaccination coverage has been satisfactory. The Committee observes 
that in three out of four districts (i.e. Latur-27. 61 %, Gadchiroli-ll.85%, Washim-52.61%) in the stat e of Maharashtra 
and even in the districts of Krishna, Nellore and Medak of Andhra Pradesh the coverage has been far from satisfactory. 
As a result thereof overall 37% of children targeted in AP and 31% children in Maharashtra could be va ccinated in the 
districts given in Annexure-XIII. Even in the State  capital of Trivandrum in Kerala the coverage was o nly 40%. The 
Committee feels that the district programme officer s had perhaps failed to grasp the guidelines of the  Centre in regard 
to this disease. The Committee observes that the re asons for such a communication gap should be ascert ained by the 
Department and appropriate measures be taken to avo id such instances in future. The Committee would li ke to be 

apprised about the latest coverage status for all t he 30 districts where the vaccination was carried o ut and whether a 
follow-up round has been ca,rried out by the Centre to determine the actual coverage of the targeted beneficiaries. 
        (Para 6.18)  
VII.   NATIONAL TB CONTROL PROGRAMME 
  From a perusal of Annexure-XV of the ATN furnishe d by the Department, the Committee finds that, for the 
period April to December, 2009, in 9 States/ UTs no expenditure has been reported by the States TB Societies on account 
of disbursement of funds to NGOs under revised Nati onal TB Control Programme. Again, in another 10 Sta tes/ UTs, 
there is a disbursement of less than Rs. 1 lakh. Th e Committee finds an uneven pattern of funds disbur sement and fails 
to understand the reasons behind such a scenario. I t is also not clear as to whether the lack of disbu rsement is on 
account of less number of VOs/ NGOs functioning in the area or due to lack of timely disbursement of funds on the part 
of the States for the above programme. The Committe e suggests that the Department should come up with a clear 
picture in this regard.    (Para 7.8)  
VIII.  NATIONAL LEPROSY ERADICATION PROGRAMME 
  The Committee is astonished by the Department’s r eply that it plans to review the matter of under-ut ilization of 
funds in an emphatic manner with the State Programm e Officers during quarterly and annual NLEP review meetings. 
From the above statement of the Department, it is c lear that there are no proper mechanisms in place t o monitor the 
funds utilization under this programme. The Committ ee would like to state that unless proper monitorin g of the 
programme is done, such lapses would continue and w ith only two years of the Mission period remaining such a trend 
does not augur well for the health of the programme. Hence it exhorts the Department to roll-out financial monitoring of 
the various schemes under the Programme so that the creases are ironed out from the beginning.  (Para 8.8)  
  The Committee observes that out of total 2856 Rec onstructive surgeries conducted on leprosy affected  persons in 
different states during 2009-10, maximum beneficiar ies are reported to be in Andhra Pradesh (617), Bih ar (94), 
Chhattisgarh (108), Gujarat (110), Karnataka (113),  MP (282), Maharashtra (451), Orissa (259), Tamil N adu (132), UP 
(376), West Bengal (101) and Delhi (132). However, as per the details furnished by the Department only  121 persons 
belonging to BPL family could avail of the incentiv e of Rs. 5000/- with 100 beneficiaries being in Kar nataka alone. This 
clearly shows that States have not been fully sensitized about this specific benefits available for leprosy patients going for 
reconstructive surgery. The Committee would like to  reiterate that all the States having higher concen tration of such 
cases need to be sensitized.   (Para 8.10)  
IX.   NATIONAL TRACHOMA AND BLINDNESS CONTROL PROGR AMME 
  The Committee observes that to ensure utilization  of funds allocated for the earmarked activities, i t is essential 
that the remedial measures identified during an ass essment of a programme be implemented at the earlie st so that 
initiatives can be brought back on to the track. The Committee finds that the Department has only passed instructions to 
the States for better financial management and the fact that it has not pursued the matter further is evident from its 
reply. The Committee suggests that with a number of  important entities like Chartered Accountant, MBAs  etc. finding 
place under the NRHM, the State Programme Management Units should be persuaded to execute the remedial measures 
so that the targets under such an important program me are achieved within a reasonable time-frame. The  Committee, 
therefore, suggests the Department to get the feedb ack from the States and formulate further remedial measures if the 
progress is below par.    (Para 9.4 & 9.5)  
  The Committee would like to be apprised of the cl arification sought from the States in this regard.  (Para 9.7)  
  The Committee feels that merely issuing routine i nstructions to the States to ensure standards of Ey e Banking to 
improve utilization of collected eyes is unlikely t o bear fruit. The Committee would expect the Depart ment to devise 
innovative means and methods to objectively assess the progress and analyse in detail the reasons for the shortfall for 
formulating appropriate remedial measures.  (Para 9.10)  

  The Department has merely furnished the figures p ertaining to Tamil Nadu and Gujarat and not comment ed on 
the reasons for low level of utilization of collect ed corneas. The Committee would have appreciated if  the Department 
had dwelt upon the reasons for low utilization and worked out remedial measures to smoothen the inadeq uacies within 
the existing arrangements and storage of the cornea s to bridge out the vast gap between the eyes donat ed and those 
utilized.      (Para 9.11)  
  The Committee understands that the two major reas ons cited for non-utilization of donated corneas ar e beyond 
the control of the eye banks but the third reason c ited in the matter could be addressed to by the Dep artment by 
development of required infrastructure. The Committ ee, accordingly, impresses upon the Department to t ake adequate 
steps to ensure optimum utilization of collected corneas.  (Para 9.13)  
X.   NATIONAL IODINE DEFICIENCY DISORDERS CONTROL P ROGRAMME 
  From a perusal of the above mentioned Annexure, t he Committee is perturbed to find that out of the 
districts surveyed for iodine deficiency, a large number were found endemic. The Committee would like the Department 
to chalk out an action plan to deal with the situation and apprise the Committee about its outcome.  
                  (Para 10.5 & 10.6)

Status of 4 Medical Colleges who have filed Court cases with regard to withdrawal of recognition  
1. Dr. B. R. Ambedkar Medical  With regard to withd rawal of recognition and show cause notice  
 College, Bangalore, Karnataka issued to the colleg e authorities, Dr. B.R. Ambedkar Medical College  
moved to High Court of Karnataka. The High Court pa ssed an order on 11.6.2009 
directing the Central Government to take a final decision within 4 months. 
The college was inspected by MCI on 6th & 7th April 2010 and the inspection report 
was considered by the Executive Committee of MCI at  its meeting held on 12th & 
13th May 2010, which noted various deficiencies wit h regard to teaching faculty, 
clinical material and infrastructural facilities. T he Executive Committee of MCI has 
recommended to the Central Government for withdrawal of recognition and stoppage 
of admissions from the academic year 2010-11. 
2. Dr. Panjabrao Alias  Withdrawal of recognition a s recommended by MCI and the show  
 Bhausaheb’s Deshmukh  cause notice issued by this Ministry was challenged by the college  
 Memorial Medical College,  authorities. The High C ourt of Bombay at Nagpur passed an order  
 Amrawati, Maharashtra on 22.9.2008 permitting the college authorities to admit the students  
as per merit. The judgment of High Court was challe nged by MCI by filing an SLP 
(No. 24195/2008) in Supreme Court. On the basis of Supreme Court Order, students 
were admitted for the academic year 2008-09. No adm issions were made in the 
academic year 2009-10 as MCI directed the college a uthorities not to make any 
admission. 
The college authorities have itself asked some more  time to rectify the deficiencies. 
The compliance report made by the college has been sent to MCI and the comments 
of MCI are still awaited. 
3. Manipal University The Manipal University Challe nged this Ministry notification vide   
 (Kasturba Medical College,  which the admission ca pacity of KMC Manipal and KMC Mangalore  
 Manipal, Karnataka Kasturba  was reduced to 150 an d 100 students respectively. High Court of  
 Medical College, Mangalore,  Karnataka vide  its two interim orders (W.P. No. 13319/2008) resto red  
 Kamataka). the admission capacity of 250 students in each KMC, Manipal and  
KMC, Mangalore. Against the order of High Court, MC I file an Special Leave to 
Appeal (No. 20018-20019/2009) in Supreme Court and Hon’ble court on 6.11.2009 
ordered that “we do not approve of the way in which the interim orders are passed by 
the High Court. We request the High Court to dispose of the matter pending before it 
at an early date at least within the next academic year. 
The Ld. Division Bench of the Hon’ble High Court of  Karnataka at Bangalore has 
finally heard the Writ Petition No.13319/2008 filed  by Manipal University & Ors. - 
Vs . - Union of India & Ors. and other connected writ petition in the month of April, 
2010 and the Judgment is reserved. 

4. People’s Medical college of  A W.P No. 5933 has been filed by People’s College of Medical Sciences  
 Medical Sciences, Bhanpur,  praying before the Hon ’ble High Court praying to direct the MCI to  
 Bhopal. make recommendations in respect of recogni tion of petitioner’s  
  medical college to Central Govt. The matter is un der consideration  
  with Board of Governors, MCI.

Annexure-XIV  
Part A (Health) Para No. 19.7  
(A)   Status of revision of CHS Rules:---  As regards status of the review/revision of CHS Ru les, it is stated that revised draft 
of CHS Rules, after concurrence of Department of Personnel and Training, were sent to UPSC on 14.7.2009 for the approval of 
the Commission. The UPSC vide  their letter dated 23.7.2009 raised certain querie s about the proposed amendments in CHS 
Rules, which were replied by this Ministry on 18.8.2009. Subsequently, the Secretary, UPSC was requested by this Ministry on 
17.9.2009 for expediting the approval of Commission  to the proposed amendments in CHS Rules. UPSC vide  their letter dated 
5.10.09 conveyed another set of queries about propo sed revised Rules. While clarifying all the queries  raised vide  letter dated 
26.10.2009, Secretary, UPSC was also requested for convening a meeting for early finalization of the R ules. Accordingly, a 
meeting was held in UPSC on 18.12.2009 under the Ch airmanship of Secretary, UPSC. The proposed amendme nts were 
discussed and UPSC asked for data about year-wise v acancies for three calendar years (2007, 2008 and 2 009) and a written 
confirmation that proposed qualifications for Speci alities/Super-Specialities are in harmony with exta nt Medical Council of 
India (MCI) Regulations. The requisite data about y ear-wise vacancies in respect of 22 Specialities wa s sent on 22.12.2009 
alongwith confirmation that the qualification mentioned in proposed Rules are as per MCI Regulations. 

  UPSC was reminded on 29.1.2010 for expediting the  clearance of amendments of CHS Rules. In response,  UPSC called 
another meeting for further discussion in the matte r. The meeting was held on 19.2.2010. Thereafter UP SC vide  letter dated 
15.3.2010 had sought further clarifications which have been sent. It would be observed from above narration that the Ministry is 
making all out efforts to get the Rules approved by UPSc. 
(B)    Dynamic Assured Career Progression Scheme (DACP) :---  On the recommendations of Vth Central Pay Commissi on, 
Dynamic Assured Career Progression (DACP) Scheme wa s introduced and implemented in respect of CHS doct ors w.e.f.  5th 
April, 2002 vide  letter No. 21/14/97-PC(H)/CHS.V. Under this Scheme , CHS doctors were granted promotion upto CMO 
(NFSG) level on time-bound basis without linkage to  vacancies. Further, consequent to: the decision ta ken by Government, at 
the time of implementation of recommendations of 6th CPC, DACP Scheme has now been extended upto SAG l evel posts vide  
O.M. No. A.45012/2/2008-CHS.V dated 29.10.2008. As of now, the CHS doctors are being granted promotion s under DACP 
scheme as per pattern given hereunder:

Annexure-XV   
Part A (Health) Para No. 20.2  
Pilot project on Prevention and Control of Human Rabies  
        Question                                      Answer 
              1                                          2 
The pilot project on control  To prevent human deat hs due to rabies a pilot project is being carried o ut in  
of human rabies and preven- five cities viz: Ahmeda bad, Bangalore, Delhi, Pune, and Madurai. NCDC is  
tion is still being run on pilot  the nodal agency to coordinate various activities under the project.  It was  
project basis taken as pilot project to test the st rategy for prevention and control of  
human rabies and develop interface with veterinary and animal husbandry department to development cons ensus strategy for 
control of rabies in animals. This would help in co nceiving programme for prevention and control of ra bies in the country in 
future. 
The project has been further extended for period of  one year i.e.  2010-11 to gain more experience on its implementat ion in 
semi-urban & rural areas and establish coordination with veterinary sector for prevention and control of rabies in animals. 
Progress made so far  

 /circle6 1865 doctors and paramedical staff have been train ed on appropriate animal bite 
management. 
 /circle6 Intradermal route of administration has been imple mented in 54 centres in pilot 
project cities. 
 /circle6 Rabies immunoglobulins are being procured and used  in pilot project cities out of the 
allocated funds. 
 /circle6 Awareness has been created in general community. 
 /circle6 One laboratory each in pilot project city has been  strengthened. 
 /circle6 Surveillance on animal bite cases has been strengt hened. 
Utilization of funds has been  Initially the projec t was proposed for Rs.8.00 crores. However, an amou nt  
nominal of Rs. 3.26 crores was allocated on final a pproval of the project.

Utilization of allocation in 11.   An amount of Rs.  160.00 lakhs has been allocated for 2010  
 2010-11 Meeting to review the activities carried out so far and develop plan of  
 action for 2010-11 was organized at NCDC. Allocatio n of funds is under process. 
This will enable to develop a consensus strategy fo r control of rabies in animals 
along with prevention and control of rabies in huma ns and will help in formulation 
of national rabies control Programme.

Annexure-XI 
(Part-B NRHM Para 5.17)  
  This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor Genera l of India for the year ended March, 2008 containing the results of the 
Performance Audit of the implemention of the "Natio nal Rural Health Mission" has been prepared for sub mission to the 
President of India under Article-151 of the Constitution. 
  The Performance Audit was conducted between April , 2008 and December, 2008 through test check of rec ord of the 
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare State and Dis tric Health Societies and health centres covering t he period 2005-06 to 
2007-08. 
 
 

 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Annexure-XI (Continued)  
  To support the immunisation programme, cold chain  mainetance was visualised in all CHCs and PHCs. Ou t of 220 
audited CHCs, the essential equipment to maintain c old chain i.e.  ice lined freezers, refrigerators and deep freezer s were 
available in 205 CHCs (93 per cent), 156 CHCs (71 p er cent) and 209 CHCs (95 per cent respectively in 21 States/UTs (details 
in Annex 6.8-A). In none of the 12 sample test checked CHCs in Bihar, was cold chain equipment available. 
  While out of 217 PHCs test checked, ice lined fre ezers, refrigerators, and deep freezers were availa ble in 110 PHCs (51 
per cent), 90 PHCs (41 per cent) and 104 PHCs (48 p er cent) respectively (details in Annex 6.8-B). How ever, none of the cold 
chain equipment was available in any of the 124 tes t checked PHCs in Haryana, Meghalaya, Madhya Prades h, Puducherry and 
West Bengal (5 States/UTs). 

  Further, the equipment available was not put to e fficient use for want of continious power supply an d due to non-
functioning non-availability of standby power sourecs. Besides, in Bihar, Lakshadweep and Uttar Prades h, 31 to 60 per cent of 
cold chain equipment was non functional. 
  The Ministry stated that GOI provided budgetary s upport for maintenance of cold chain equipment to t he States/UTs 
under Strengthening of Routine Immunization as well as supplied spare parts. Further, in case of disruption of power supply, the 
GOI also provided for POL for generator for PHC/CHC  for alternate power supply which can be used for m aintance of cold 
chain. 
  However, it appears that SHSs were not utilizing the resources provided by the Ministry effectively.  The absance of cold 
chain management could adversely impact on the effe ctiveness of the Universal Immunization Programme, a high priority area 
under the Mission. 
  Each Sub Centre under the NRHM was to be run by t wo Auxiliary Nursing Midwives (ANM, female) and a Multipurpose 
worker (MPW, male). The Mission aimed to ensure two  ANMs at 30 per cent Sub Centres by 2007 and 60 per  cent by 2008 
with the second ANM being appointed on a contract b asis . While the ANMs were to be paid out of centra l grants, the MPWs 
were to be paid by the state Government. Among samp le units, 116 sub Sub Centres (9 per cent) of 20 St ates/UTs were 
functioning without an ANM. At 992 Sub Centres (77 per cent) of 29 States/UTs two ANMs were not posted  and in Himachal 
Pradesh, Karnataka, Madhya Pradesh, Manipur, Meghal aya, Sikkim, Tamil Nadu, Uttar Pradesh, West Bengal  and 
Lakshadweep none of Sub Centres had two ANMs. The deployment of MPWs was inadequate and 775 Sub Centres (60 per cent 
of 27 States/UTs had no MPW. In Bihar, Uttar Prades h, Lakshadweep, Chandigarh and Puducherry none of t he test checked 
Sub Centres had an MPW. In contrast, in Meghalaya, Mizoram, Sikkim and Daman & Diu all the tested sub Centres  had an 
MPW. The State-wise status of non-availability of required staff at Sub Center is detailed in Annex 6.9.
